## Page 1


Higher categories of bordisms with geometric structures
Daniel Grady
gradydaniel.com
Wichita State University
Dmitri Pavlov
dmitripavlov.org
Texas Tech University
Abstract. We introduce a system of axioms that uniquely defines an (∞, d)-category of bordisms equipped with
geometric data. The underlying manifolds of these bordisms may be smooth, complex, super, or formal smooth
manifolds, as well as any class of manifolds satisfying conditions specified in this paper. We develop a general notion
of a field on a manifold, encompassing structures such as Riemannian metrics, principal bundles with connection,
conformal structures, and traditional tangential structures. Using this framework, we construct a symmetric mon-
oidal (∞, d)-category of bordisms with prescribed underlying manifolds and fields, and prove that it satisfies our
axioms.

1. Introduction
This paper, together with [2020.e, 2026.c, 2021.c], is the first of a series devoted to the study of nontopological
extended functorial field theories (FFTs), culminating in a proof of a geometric version of the cobordism hypothesis
(Grady–Pavlov [2021.c]).
For a geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category C, we are interested in the moduli space of functorial field
theories
Fun⊗(BordF
d , C).
1
arXiv:2605.03453v1  [math.AT]  5 May 2026


## Page 2


2
A direct analysis of this object would be incredibly complicated, due to the large amount of structure imposed on
both the source and target of the mapping object. Following the topological cobordism hypothesis of Baez–Dolan
[1995], and Lurie [2009.c], we seek to identify the above moduli object in simpler terms. The end result (Grady–Pav-
lov [2021.c, Theorem 1.0.4]) is a classification theorem that is similar to statement of the cobordism hypothesis, as
formulated by Lurie [2009.c, Theorem 2.4.18].
The framework of functorial quantum field theory was proposed by Segal [2004.a], following Witten’s axiomatic
approach to path integrals in the context of a two-dimensional conformal field theory. Segal’s definition can be
summarized as follows: a d-dimensional quantum field theory Z is a monoidal functor
Z: Bordd →Vectk,
where Bordd is the d-dimensional bordism category (possibly equipped with additional structures), with monoidal
structure given by disjoint union of bordisms, and Vectk is the category of vector spaces over a field k.
Thus, an FFT assigns to an object in Bordd (i.e., a closed (d −1)-dimensional smooth manifold Md−1) a vector
space Z(Md−1). This vector space is to be thought of as the vector space of quantum states (hence, emphasizing
the Schrödinger picture). Following Segal and Atiyah [1988], Freed [1992, 1993.b] and Lawrence [1993.c] proposed
extended topological quantum field theories, which incorporate bordisms with corners of codimension 2 and higher.
In this paper, we introduce a version of an extended bordism category that is capable of handling nontopological
fields on bordisms. We also set the stage for the follow-up paper (Grady–Pavlov [2020.e]) on locality of extended field
theories, by introducing axioms for extended geometric bordism categories and proving that our definition satisfies
these axioms. The locality result in Grady–Pavlov [2020.e] holds for any extended bordism category satisfying the
axioms, which makes the proof independent of the construction of the bordism category presented here.
The first definition of a (nonextended) geometric bordism category is due to Segal [2004.a], building on Milnor’s
definition [1965, Section 1] in the topological case. A more elaborate bordism category capable of handling various
geometries was constructed by Stolz and Teichner [2004.b, 2011.a] in their study of supersymmetric Euclidean field
theories. Despite these constructions, very little work has been done in the extended setting. One reason for this
is that a rigorous definition (in the topological case) of an extended bordism category has not appeared until Lurie
[2009.c, Section 2.2] and Calaque–Scheimbauer [2015.c, Section 5]. Moreover, to talk about the nontopological case,
one needs to parameterize the bordism category over various flavors of manifolds (e.g., smooth, holomorphic, super),
and this adds another layer of complexity that has not yet been explored in the literature in the extended case.
To incorporate a variety of geometric fields on bordisms, such as Riemannian metrics, orientations, spin and
string structures, as well as more complicated higher geometric fields such as principal bundles with connection or
geometric string structures, we introduce the notion of a field stack. Motivated by fields and their usage in physics,
we make the following three observations.
(1) Given a field on a bordism, we should be able to restrict the field to an open subset of the bordism.
(2) Given fields on elements of an open cover of a bordism that are compatible on intersections and higher
intersections (possibly up to a gauge transformation), we should be able to reconstruct a field on the entire
bordism.
(3) A functorial field theory should map smooth, holomorphic, or super families of bordisms and fields to
corresponding families in the target category. Such families can be encoded by varying both the bordism
data and the field data with respect to some parameter space. We expect that the values of the field theory
should pull back along a map of parameter spaces.
Thus, a class of fields should give rise to a sheaf (or a higher sheaf) on the site of families of d-dimensional
manifolds with fiberwise open embeddings as morphisms, and it is natural to consider any sheaf on this site as a
legitimate space of fields. The definition of a field stack is given in Section 5.1. The following list provides examples
of geometric structures that can be encoded in our setting.
• Smooth maps to a fixed target manifold, i.e., sigma models.
• Riemannian and pseudo-Riemannian metrics, possibly with restrictions on Ricci or sectional curvature.
• Super-Euclidean structures of Stolz–Teichner [2011.a, Section 2.5].
• Topological tangential structures, such as orientation, spin structure, string structure, and framing.
• Principal G-bundles with connection.
• Conformal, complex, symplectic, contact, and Kähler structures.
• Bundle n-gerbes with connection.
• Geometric spin, string, and fivebrane structures (Fiorenza–Schreiber–Stasheff [2010, Definition 6.3.1]).
• Immersions into a fixed target manifold.
• Foliations, possibly equipped with additional structures such as transversal metrics.


## Page 3


3
Continuing with observation (3) above, we will need to make sense of families of objects and morphisms in both
the source and the target category for field theories. This leads us to the notion of a geometric symmetric monoidal
(∞, d)-category (Definition 3.1.8.(1)). In particular, for a field stack F, we get a geometric symmetric monoidal
(∞, d)-category of bordisms BordF
d (Definition 5.3.7).
Our extended bordism category is different from the definitions of Lurie [2009.c, Definition Sketch 2.4.17],
Calaque–Scheimbauer [2015.c, Definition 9.10], and Schommer-Pries [2017.d, Definition 5.8], see Conjecture 5.3.23,
though. For experts, we list some of the most important differences.
• We allow nontopological fields on bordisms, such as Riemannian metrics or principal bundles with connec-
tions.
• Bordisms come in geometric families, parameterized by a structured manifold (e.g., smooth, holomorphic,
super), and functorial field theories map families of bordisms to families in the target category.
• Since fields glue along open subsets, sources and targets of bordisms must be equipped with two-sided
collars that themselves carry fields. The actual codimension 1 manifold can then be extracted from such
a collar as h−1(0) for a height function h: M →R that has 0 as its regular value. We do not keep track
of such a height function h. Instead, we keep track of the cut induced by h, defined as the triple of sets
(h−1(−∞, 0), h−1(0), h−1(0, ∞)) (Definition 5.2.1).
• We allow cuts in a composable sequence of bordisms to overlap (Figure 2), but not to intersect transver-
sally. These features allow us to implement strict globularity in our bordism categories (Examples 5.2.14
and 5.4.3). This leads to simplifications compared to the usual approaches involving cylindrical neighbor-
hoods, which produce weakly equivalent bordism categories.
• We encode the homotopy type of the diffeomorphism group of a bordism as a smooth set, i.e., a presheaf on
C∞Cart. This presheaf-theoretic approach enables the use of stalks (Remark 2.2.6), leading to significantly
more efficient proofs. By encoding the moduli spaces of bordisms as smooth sets, we avoid using Whitney’s
C∞-topology and the resulting technicalities. We do not embed bordisms into a contractible space like R∞
or attempt to topologize the resulting set.
• We do not require bordism categories to be fibrant in some model structure (Remarks 3.1.10 and 5.3.19).
All constructions in this series of papers respect weak equivalences and work equally well with fibrant or
nonfibrant objects. Furthermore, fibrancy is useful when mapping into an object, whereas a functorial field
theory maps out of a bordism category. The latter type of maps benefits from a cofibrancy condition, and
all objects are cofibrant in the relevant model category. Removing the fibrancy condition leads to further
simplifications in the proofs. For example, the embedded bordism category admits a simplified description
(Definition 6.2.1) as a presheaf of sets, which does not hold for a fibrant version because of Remark 6.2.6.
Working with a nonfibrant bordism category does not require us to add h-cobordisms to the moduli stack
of objects, which would further complicate the proof.
• We use embedded bordism categories (Definition 6.2.1) as a fundamental building block of bordism categories
with geometric structures. These categories are encoded as presheaves of (smooth) sets (as opposed to
(smooth) simplicial sets) on the category GCart × Γ × ∆×d. This allows for further simplifications in the
proof of locality (Grady–Pavlov [2020.e]) and the geometric cobordism hypothesis (Grady–Pavlov [2021.c]).
The above notions lead to a natural definition of a geometric extended functorial field theory.
Definition 1.0.1. Let T be a fibrant geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category (Definition 3.1.8.(1)).
A
T-valued extended field theory Z with field stack F ∈Fieldd is a map
Z: BordF
d →T
in GCat⊗
∞,d.
In the case of representable field stacks F, i.e., representable objects in Fieldd = PSh∆(GEmbd), it turns out
that our bordism category simplifies to a very concrete and easy-to-understand category of embedded bordisms
(Definition 6.2.1). For the proof of the locality property in Grady–Pavlov [2020.e], we first reduce to the case
of representable presheaves. We then leverage the explicit description of this category of embedded bordisms to
decompose bordisms into small pieces subordinate to an open cover.
The reduction to representable presheaves allows us to treat any bordism category that satisfies the following
axioms.

Theorem 1.0.4 (Theorem 6.2.8). There exists a geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category of bordisms,
unique up to a contractible choice. In particular, the category Bordd of Definition 5.3.7 satisfies Definition 1.0.2.
If the field stack F ∈Fieldd is a presheaf of sets, the resulting geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category
BordF
d is a geometric symmetric monoidal (d + 1, d)-category, with (d + 1)-morphisms being structure-preserving
diffeomorphisms of d-bordisms. The truncation of this category to a (d, d)-category has as d-morphisms structure-
preserving diffeomorphism classes of d-bordisms, mimicking the traditional constructions of bordism categories.
In the proof of the geometric cobordism hypothesis (Grady–Pavlov [2021.c]), we will use another variant of
the category of bordisms which incorporates the smooth set of diffeomorphisms of bordisms. As a consequence,
this yields a genuine (∞, d)-category of bordisms, with (d + 2)-morphisms being isotopies of diffeomorphisms of d-
bordisms, (d+3)-morphisms being isotopies of isotopies, and so on. Including isotopies is crucial, since without them
the category of bordisms does not have all duals (see Examples 5.5.5 and 5.5.7). For additional motivation, see Lurie
[2009.c, Section 1.4] for an exposition in the topological case. We denote the geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-
category of bordisms with isotopies by BordF
d , where F is a field stack with isotopies in the sense of Definition 5.1.3.
We remark that the proof of locality in Grady–Pavlov [2020.e] works for both bordism categories, with or without
isotopies.
The bordism category with isotopies satisfies an analogous set of axioms as Definition 1.0.2.

1.1. An overview of the series. Below we provide a summary of how each paper in the series contributes to the
problem of computing moduli spaces of functorial field theories. In each paper, we use certain model categories that
present corresponding (∞, 1)-categories or (∞, d)-categories. These specific models are chosen for technical reasons
and allow us to simplify certain arguments. To emphasize the key conceptual aspects, the summary below omits
details of homotopy-theoretic constructions.


## Page 5


5
1.1.1. Higher categories of bordisms (present work). In this first paper, we introduce the extended geometric
bordism categories BordF
d and BordF
d and define the moduli spaces of extended geometric functorial field theories
FFTF
d,C and FFTF
d,C.
The bordism categories introduced in this work are capable of encoding a large class of
structures on bordisms, both in the field content and in the structure of the bordism itself. In particular, we allow
for bordisms to be equipped with a supermanifold or holomorphic structure. In addition, we allow for structured
families of such bordisms, such as superfamilies or holomorphic families. To encode this type of structured bordism,
we introduce the notion of a fibered geometric site GEmbd and fibered geometric site with isotopies GEmbd, where
the G and G refer to the geometric structure.
We also take a first step in describing this extended geometric bordism category in simpler terms. For brevity,
we work with the isotopy variants below, with the nonisotopy versions obtained in a similar manner. Theorem 6.1.3
asserts that the functor
F 7→BordF
d ,
F ∈PSh(∞,1)(GEmbd)
is homotopy cocontinuous in its argument F, which is an ∞-presheaf on a certain site GEmbd of families of structured
d-dimensional manifolds with fiberwise open embeddings as morphisms. Every ∞-presheaf can be written as a
homotopy colimit over representables Yq, with q ∈GEmbd. Using formal properties of the mapping object, we get
that

This is an interesting result, but not very useful in practice on its own, since the ∞-presheaf FFTd,C is still
much too complicated to compute directly. We emphasize that at this stage, the mapping object Map(F, FFTd,C)
computes maps of ∞-presheaves, not ∞-sheaves.

d ,

the homotopy descent condition, i.e., is an ∞-sheaf. Thus, the mapping object of ∞-presheaves Map(F, FFTd,C)
can be computed as the mapping object of ∞-sheaves:
MapPSh(F, FFTd,C) ≃MapSh(F, FFTd,C).

## Page 6


6
In many practical examples, the identification of the O(d)-action on C× can proceed as follows. There is a simple
guess as to what the action of O(d) can be for a particular choice of C, e.g., when C = BdU(1) or C is a delooping
of the category of Hilbert spaces. To show that this guess D coincides with the O(d)-action provided by the above
construction, we construct a map
ψ: D →FFT
YpU
d,C ,
which in practice amounts to writing down a particularly simple functorial field theory. For example, in the case of
C = BdU(1) such a field theory can be given by integrating a differential d-form over a d-bordism. Once the map ψ
is set up, it remains to show that the composition e ◦ψ is an equivalence of ∞-sheaves on GCart, which in practice
is often tautological given the construction of D.
Therefore, we can compute the moduli space of functorial field theories as
MapO(d)(evd F, C×) ≃MapO(d)(evd F, D).
The computation of the latter object is unique to the class of field theories at hand, with some examples presented
in Grady–Pavlov [2021.c], Kenig–Pavlov [2026.d], and further examples forthcoming.
1.2. Applications. The main theorems of [2020.e, 2021.c] rely on Theorems 1.0.4 and 1.0.6. We point out some
additional applications of these results.
• The codescent theorem [2020.e] enables a simple proof (forthcoming work in preparation) of a geometric
fully extended variant of the Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss theorem [2006.b]. Another proof of the same
result is presented in Grady–Pavlov [2021.c, Theorem 5.0.5], using the geometric cobordism hypothesis.
• In Grady–Pavlov [2020.e, Theorem 7.2.7], we prove a conjecture originally posed by Stolz and Teichner
[2011.a]: concordance classes of extended field theories admit a classifying space (in the sense of Brown’s
representability theorem).
This uses the codescent theorem of [2020.e] together with the smooth Oka
principle established in Berwick-Evans–Boavida–Pavlov [2019].
• In Example 5.4.9 we give a complete definition of fully extended 2|1-Euclidean field theories. In a forth-
coming paper, we will use the geometric cobordism hypothesis to classify such field theories in terms of
representation theory of the 2|1-Euclidean group.
Prerequisites. We assume familiarity with the language of simplicial homotopy theory and model categories, in
particular, the following topics.
• Simplicial homotopy theory, including simplicial sets, simplicial maps, simplicial weak equivalences, nerves
of categories, Quillen’s Theorem A, simplicial categories. See Goerss–Jardine [1999.a] and Richter [2020.b].
• Model categories, including model structures, Quillen adjunctions, injective and projective model structures
on presheaves, monoidal and cartesian model categories, combinatorial model categories. See Hovey [1999.b],
Hirschhorn [2002.b], Barwick [2007], Lurie [2017.a, Appendix A], Cisinski [2019, Section 7.11], and the survey
of Balchin [2021.a].
• Homotopy limits and colimits. See Bousfield–Kan [1972, Chapters XI and XII], Hirschhorn [2002.b, Chap-
ter 18], Shulman [2006.c], Riehl [2014.a, Chapters 5–8].
• Grothendieck fibrations in sets, groupoids, and categories. See Borceux [1994.b, Chapter 8], Vistoli [2005.b],
Johnstone [2002.a, Section B1.3].
We also make use of the following formalisms, for which we review the necessary background and provide appropriate
references as needed.
• Segal’s Γ-objects.
See Segal [1974.a, Sections 1 and 2], Bousfield–Friedlander [1978, Sections 3 and 5],
Schwede [1999.d, Section 1], Richter [2020.b, Section 14.3].
• Complete Segal spaces (Rezk [1998.b]) and n-fold complete Segal spaces (Barwick [2005.a, Section 2.3]).
• Simplicial presheaves and descent. See Dugger–Hollander–Isaksen [2002.d], Jardine [2015.a], Glass–Mini-
chiello [2026.a].

2. Homotopy theory and higher categories
As discussed in the introduction, the main goal of this paper is to establish a model for the extended bordism
category that is capable of handling geometric structures. Such an extended bordism category should have the
following basic structure.
(1) It should be a higher category with k-morphisms for all k > 0 such that for every k > d, all k-morphisms
are invertible.
(2) It should be symmetric monoidal, with coherences for the monoidal structure.
(3) It should allow for geometric families for objects or k-morphisms, for example smooth or holomorphic
families.
We now have well-developed machinery that can encode (1) and (2). The encoding of symmetric monoidal struc-
tures follows Segal [1974.a, Definition 1.2]. The encoding of categorical composition follows the same idea of Segal,
as developed by Dwyer–Kan–Smith [1989.b, Section 7] (Segal (∞, 1)-categories), Tamsamani [1996, Definition 1.3.2]
(Segal n-categories), Hirschowitz–Simpson [1998.a, Section 2] and Pellissier [2003] (Segal (∞, n)-categories), Rezk
[1998.b, Section 6] (complete Segal spaces), Barwick [2005.a, Scholium 2.3.18] (complete n-fold Segal spaces), and
Lurie [2009.b, Section 1] (globular complete n-fold Segal spaces).
To encode (3), we will parametrize our higher monoidal categories by objects of a site GCart (Definition 4.1.12).
We will want these parametrized higher categories to satisfy a homotopy coherent sheaf gluing property with respect
to open covers so that families of objects and k-morphisms can be assembled from local data. The encoding of
geometric families was influenced by Stolz–Teichner [2004.b, 2011.a]. The use of the cartesian site in this context
was proposed by Urs Schreiber (see, e.g., Fiorenza–Schreiber–Stasheff [2010, Appendix]). The site GCart is very
general and allows for a variety of geometric structure on cartesian spaces. In particular, we can encode smooth,
super, and complex structures on cartesian spaces.
To encode invertible k-morphisms for k > d, we present the ∞-groupoid of such morphisms (with objects
being d-morphisms, 1-morphisms being (d + 1)-morphisms, etc.) as a simplicial set. We also find it convenient to
talk about isotopies (i.e., R-parametrized smooth homotopies) of k-morphisms, which leads to the structure of a
smooth simplicial set (Notation 2.2.2). From a theoretical viewpoint, smooth simplicial sets are Quillen equivalent to
simplicial sets (Proposition 2.2.5), via an appropriate analog of the singular complex functor. Thus, adding isotopies
does not increase the level of generality of our theory. From a practical viewpoint, it is convenient to treat isotopies
in this manner, since we can talk about stalks of isotopies (see Remark 2.2.6). This technique is exploited in the proof
of the locality property in Grady–Pavlov [2020.e]. Similar ideas were used by Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss
[2006.b, Sections 2.2 and 2.3].
2.1. Geometric, symmetric monoidal, and higher categorical structure. Recall the simplex category ∆,
whose objects are nonempty, totally ordered sets [d] = {0, . . . , d}, and whose morphisms are order-preserving
maps. Recall also Segal’s category Γ [1974.a, Definition 1.1] (see also Bousfield–Friedlander [1978, Section 3] and
Schwede [1999.d, Section 1]), whose opposite category has finite pointed sets ⟨ℓ⟩= {∗, 1, . . . , ℓ} as objects and
basepoint-preserving functions as morphisms. Finally, recall the category C∞Cart (Definition 2.1.8.(2), see also
Fiorenza–Schreiber–Stasheff [2010, Definition 3.1.1]) whose objects are cartesian spaces Rn and morphisms are
smooth functions. More generally, we can use any category GCart that satisfies certain properties making it similar
to C∞Cart (Definition 4.1.12), see Definitions 4.1.19 and 4.1.20. The basic building blocks for geometric symmetric
monoidal (∞, d)-categories lie in the product category
(2.1.1)
GCart × Γ × ∆×d × ∆.
Each category captures an independent aspect of the objects being constructed. To include isotopies of higher
morphisms, we also consider the product category
(2.1.2)
GCart × Γ × ∆×d × C∞Cart × ∆,
with the factor C∞Cart playing a completely different role than the first factor GCart. We elaborate on this in
Section 2.1.10. We now offer conceptual interpretations for each component.
2.1.3. The category ∆×d. Here, an object is a multisimplex m = ([m1], [m2], . . . , [md]) ∈∆×d, which can be
thought of as indexing a composable grid of morphisms, with mi composed morphisms in the ith direction. For
example, for d = 2, there are two composition directions. If m1 = m2 = 4, we obtain a grid


## Page 9


9
A single square represents a 2-morphism, while edges represent 1-morphisms and vertices represent objects. These
2-morphisms can be composed in two directions, thereby forming a grid. Simplicial presheaves on ∆×d admit a
model structure whose fibrant objects are d-fold Segal spaces. Such a simplicial presheaf F encodes an (∞, d)-
category C in the following manner. Given a multisimplex m, the value F(m) is an ∞-groupoid whose objects are
diagrams in C of the above shape, morphisms are natural isomorphisms, 2-morphisms are natural 2-isomorphisms,
etc. The structure of an (∞, d)-category can be reconstructed from the knowledge of such diagrams. For examples,
objects in C can be reconstructed by evaluating at m = ([0], . . . , [0]) and taking the set of objects (i.e., vertices) of
the resulting ∞-groupoid.
For constructions that include symmetric monoidal and geometric structures, see Definitions 3.1.3 and 3.1.8.
References on d-fold Segal spaces include Barwick [2005.a, Scholium 2.3.18], Lurie [2009.b, Section 1], Lurie [2009.c,
Section 2.1], Barwick–Schommer-Pries [2011.b, Section 14], Calaque–Scheimbauer [2015.c, Section 2].
2.1.4. The category Γ. This category captures the symmetric monoidal structure. An object ⟨ℓ⟩∈Γ is a finite
set {∗, 1, 2, . . . , ℓ} and a morphism A →B in Γ is a function f: B →A satisfying f(∗) = ∗. This category encodes
the symmetric monoidal structure as follows. Consider a Γ-object X: Γop →sSet. Denote by ϕ⟨ℓ⟩: ⟨ℓ⟩→⟨1⟩the
function that sends i to 1, for all i such that 1 ≤i ≤ℓ. Then ϕ⟨ℓ⟩yields, by functoriality, a map
X(ϕ⟨ℓ⟩): X⟨ℓ⟩→X⟨1⟩.
We also have maps δi: ⟨ℓ⟩→⟨1⟩that send i to 1 and j to ∗for j ̸= i. They induce a map
δ⟨ℓ⟩= (X(δ1), . . . , X(δℓ)): X⟨ℓ⟩→X⟨1⟩×ℓ.
The multiplicative structure is given by the following zigzag, where the left leg is a weak equivalence, so can be
(formally) inverted:
X⟨1⟩×ℓ
δ⟨ℓ⟩
←−−−−−X⟨ℓ⟩
X(ϕ⟨ℓ⟩)
−−−−−→X⟨1⟩.
The space X⟨ℓ⟩can be thought of as the space of ℓ-tuples that can be multiplied. The map X(ϕ⟨ℓ⟩) then performs
the multiplication. The map δ⟨ℓ⟩extracts the individual components of an ℓ-tuple. Segal’s definition of a Γ-object
requires the map δ⟨ℓ⟩to be a weak equivalence, which formalizes the idea that any ℓ-tuple can be deformed to an
ℓ-tuple that can be multiplied, and the space of such deformations is contractible. The elegance of Segal’s method is
that it circumvents the need for explicitly keeping track of coherent homotopies in the symmetric monoidal structure,
although these are implicitly contained in the above equivalence. For more information, see Segal [1974.a]. The
specific case of symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories was examined by Toën [2000.b, Section 2.2] and Barwick
[2005.a, Section 3.1].
2.1.5. The category GCart. The categories C∞Cart and, more generally, GCart encode parametrizing families of
bordisms.
Definition 2.1.6. (Johnstone [2002.a, Definition C2.1.1], Minichiello [2025.a, Definition 2.6].) A site is a category
equipped with a coverage. An enriched site is an enriched category together with a coverage on its underlying
category.
The following definition is designed so that for a fixed smooth manifold N, the class of manifolds M that admit
a closed inclusion into N is a set. Furthermore, the full subcategory of the slice category C∞Man/N on included
submanifolds is a poset, in particular, every isomorphism is identity.
Definition 2.1.7. A closed inclusion of manifolds is a closed smooth map f: M →N that is an embedding of a
submanifold and whose underlying map of sets is an inclusion of sets.
Definition 2.1.8. We define the following small sites.
(1) The site C∞Man is defined as follows.
Objects are smooth manifolds.
Morphisms are smooth maps.
Covering families are open covers.
(2) The small site C∞Cart is defined as follows. Objects are manifolds in C∞Man diffeomorphic to Rn for some
n ≥0 and admit a closed inclusion of manifolds (Definition 2.1.7) into Rm for some (necessarily unique)
m ≥0. Morphisms are smooth maps. Covering families are good open covers, defined as open covers in
which every finite intersection is empty or diffeomorphic to some Rn.


## Page 10


10
Remark 2.1.9. The condition requiring objects of C∞Cart to admit a closed inclusion into Rm for some m ≥0
singles out a small subcategory of the large category of smooth manifolds. Both m and the inclusion map can be
recovered from the object. Therefore, we do not record them as part of the data of an object.
The functor category Fun(C∞Cartop, D) can be thought of as the category of smoothly parametrized objects of D
over cartesian spaces. As a basic example, a smooth manifold M yields a functor M: C∞Cartop →Set, which maps
a cartesian space U to the set of smooth maps C∞(U, M). For more information about sheaves on this site, see
Fiorenza–Schreiber–Stasheff [2010, Appendix] or Sati–Schreiber [2020.d, Section 2.1 (arXiv); 8.1 (book)].
For applications (including the Stolz–Teichner program), we find it convenient to generalize the site C∞Cart to
accommodate manifolds with additional structures such as supermanifolds, complex manifolds, or formal manifolds.
We will denote this generalized site by GCart (Definition 4.1.12). The site C∞Cart of cartesian spaces will serve as
our primary example throughout the text. The reader who is less familiar with the theory of supermanifolds may
restrict to the case where GCart = C∞Cart throughout.
2.1.10. The categories encoding higher invertible morphisms. In addition to the categories GCart, Γ, and
∆×d, which encode geometric, symmetric monoidal, and d-categorical structures, respectively, we also need to
encode the (∞, d)-categorical structure. Traditionally, this is achieved by incorporating the copy of ∆appearing in
(2.1.1), thereby enriching constructions over simplicial sets, which encode higher homotopies between d-morphisms.
Alternatively, one could use other models for ∞-groupoids. One convenient choice is the category of simplicial
presheaves PSh∆(C∞Cart) with the injective R-local model structure (Proposition 2.2.5). This model allows us
to encode higher invertible morphisms using the geometry of cartesian spaces, as opposed to the combinatorics of
simplices.
More precisely, there are three ways in which ∞-groupoids enter the construction of the bordism category.
• Higher gauge transformations of fields form an ∞-groupoid, presented as a simplicial set (Definition 5.1.1).
• Bordisms are composed by gluing open neighborhoods of the core (Notation 5.2.17). These open neighbor-
hoods and their embeddings form a category. Taking the nerve of this category corresponds to quotienting
by the equivalence relation that identifies two bordisms if they coincide on a smaller open neighborhood;
that is, we pass to the germ of the core.
• The isotopy space of cuts (Definition 5.2.19) combined with the isotopy space of diffeomorphisms of bordisms
determines the (∞, d)-category structure (Definition 5.3.16).
The first type of ∞-groupoid is present throughout the entire paper.
In the case where the field stack is
representable, the second type of ∞-groupoid is 0-truncated and is eliminated during the transition to the embedded
bordism category in Proposition 6.2.4. The third type of ∞-groupoid uses the model via presheaves of simplicial
sets on the site C∞Cart to give a smooth space of isotopies of cuts.
It is advantageous not to use simplicial sets in the third case. This enables us to pass to stalks in the proof of
locality of extended field theories (Grady–Pavlov [2020.e]) and the geometric cobordism hypothesis (Grady–Pavlov
[2021.c]). (See Remark 2.2.6.) This technique would not be available to us if we were to use ordinary simplicial
enrichments. Instead of enriching in the model category sSet, we use a Quillen equivalent model category C∞sSet
(Proposition 2.2.5) of smooth simplicial sets, defined as presheaves of simplicial sets on the site C∞Cart.
2.2. Enriching categories. We will make use of internal homs and enrichments of presheaves on monoidal cate-
gories, such as GCart with its cartesian structure or Γ with its smash product structure. We introduce the following
systematic notation for enriched presheaves.
Notation 2.2.1. Let V be a monoidal category, C be a small category, and D be a V-enriched small category.

PSh(D, V) := Fun(Dop, V).
• We denote the V-valued mapping object in a V-enriched category by

• If V is a cocomplete monoidal category, we denote by ιV the cocontinuous functor
ιV: Set →V

Next, we recall some basic properties of the category C∞sSet. An important feature of this category is that it
admits a model structure that makes it Quillen equivalent to the category of simplicial sets. Thus, the category of
smooth simplicial sets C∞sSet can be used as another model for the (∞, 1)-category of spaces. This model is used
to encode isotopies (as explained in Section 2.1.10). We start with some basic definitions.

In this paper, almost all model categories used are left Bousfield localizations of injective model structures on
presheaves. The injective model structure has the advantage that all objects are cofibrant, which implies that all
left Quillen functors are automatically derived. This motivates the following.
Proposition 2.2.5. The category
C∞sSet = PSh∆(C∞Cart)
of smooth simplicial sets (Notation 2.2.2) admits a cartesian combinatorial model structure C∞sSetsing,inj, hence-
forth denoted by C∞sSet. Its weak equivalences coincide with the weak equivalences of Proposition 2.2.4, and its
cofibrations are monomorphisms. The resulting Quillen adjunctions
sSet

/ C∞sSetsing,inj

are Quillen equivalences.
Proof. Proposition 2.2.4 constructs a cartesian combinatorial model structure on C∞sSet with the same weak
equivalences, but a smaller class of cofibrations.
Barwick [2007, Theorem 2.16] shows that the class of mono-
morphisms is cofibrantly generated by a set of monomorphisms. The Smith recognition theorem (Barwick [2007,
Proposition 2.2]) implies the existence of a model structure, provided that the intersection of weak equivalences
and monomorphisms is closed under cobase changes and transfinite compositions. The latter condition follows from
Pavlov [2022.b, Theorem 12.7] by setting V = sSet and proceeding as follows. Monomorphisms in C∞sSet are
objectwise h-cofibrations, because h-cofibrations in sSet coincide with monomorphisms. Objectwise h-cofibrations
in C∞sSet are h-cofibrations. A cobase change of an h-cofibration is a homotopy cobase change because C∞sSet
is left proper. Homotopy cobase changes of weak equivalences are weak equivalences. Transfinite compositions of
weak equivalences are weak equivalences because they can be expressed as filtered colimits.
We now prove the cartesian property. The terminal object is cofibrant because all objects are cofibrant. Cofi-
brations are closed under the pushout product operation, as monomorphisms of sets are also closed under pushout
products. As previously shown, a cobase change of an acyclic cofibration is an acyclic cofibration. The product of
a weak equivalence and an identity map is a weak equivalence. This is because weak equivalences in C∞sSet are
created by the singular functor to simplicial sets, which preserves products and weak equivalences. Thus, by the
2-out-of-3 property for weak equivalences, the pushout product of a cofibration and an acyclic cofibration is a weak
equivalence. The Quillen adjunctions in the statement are Quillen equivalences, which follows from Proposition 2.2.4
and the fact that the two model structures on C∞sSet share the same weak equivalences.
□
Remark 2.2.6. By Pavlov [2022.b, Proposition 12.5], a stalkwise weak equivalence of simplicial presheaves is a
weak equivalence in the model structures C∞sSetsing,proj (Proposition 2.2.4) and C∞sSetsing,inj (Proposition 2.2.5).
The converse is false because the map Rn →R0 is not a stalkwise weak equivalence; however, its singular complex
is a weak equivalence of simplicial sets. Thus, the model structures C∞sSetsing,proj and C∞sSetsing,inj are not Quillen
equivalent to the Čech-local model structures (projective or injective) C∞sSetˇCech,proj and C∞sSetˇCech,inj.

2.3. Categories of presheaves and their left Bousfield localizations. In this section, we recall the theorem
of Smith on the existence of left Bousfield localizations of model categories and enriched model categories. This
will set the stage for the construction of the model category of geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories in
Section 3.1, obtained via a left Bousfield localization.
Proposition 2.3.1. (Smith; Barwick [2007, Theorem 2.16, Proposition 4.50], Lurie [2017.a, Proposition A.2.8.2].)
Suppose V is a combinatorial model category and C is a small category.
The category PSh(C, V) of V-valued
presheaves on C admits a unique injective model structure such that a natural transformation t: F →G is a
cofibration (respectively weak equivalence) if for every c ∈C the map tc: F(c) →G(c) is a cofibration (respectively
weak equivalence) in V. The injective model structure is combinatorial. If V is a monoidal model category, then
PSh(C, V) is a V-enriched model category. If V is a cartesian model category, then so is PSh(C, V). If V is left proper
or tractable, then so is PSh(C, V).
In particular, there are combinatorial left proper injective model structures
PSh∆(C) = PSh(C, sSet)inj
and
C∞PSh∆(C) = PSh(C, C∞sSetsing,inj)inj
(Proposition 2.2.5). The model structure on PSh∆(C) is sSet-enriched and the one on C∞PSh∆(C) is C∞sSet-
enriched.
Proof. Cofibrations and acyclic cofibrations in injective model structures are defined objectwise, so the axioms for
V-enrichments and cartesian model categories can also be verified objectwise.
□
Remark 2.3.2. The injective model structure on Γ-spaces is not a monoidal model structure. It suffices to exhibit
a Γ-space X and a weak equivalence f: A →B of Γ-spaces such that f ∧X is not a weak equivalence. Fix m > 0
and consider the representable Γ-space Y⟨m⟩of ⟨m⟩with the canonical action of Σm via automorphisms of ⟨m⟩.
Set X = Y⟨m⟩/Σm. Take B to be the terminal Γ-space and A the Γ-space given by composing the representable
presheaf of ⟨m⟩with the functor from sets to simplicial sets that sends a set S to the nerve of the contractible
groupoid with S as the set of objects. Then f ∧Y⟨m⟩/Σm ∼= (f ∧Y⟨m⟩)/Σm ∼= f(−∧⟨m⟩)/Σm. Evaluating at ⟨1⟩,
we get the map f(⟨m⟩)/Σm, whose domain is not contractible, but the codomain is. Therefore, f ∧X is not a weak
equivalence even though f is.
Because of Remark 2.3.2 we need a different model structure on Γ-objects. A convenient choice is given by a
Reedy-type model structure like in Reedy [1974.b], but replacing the indexing category ∆with Γ. Such a model
structure was first constructed by Bousfield–Friedlander [1978, Theorem 3.5]. Its monoidality was established by
Lydakis [1999.c, Theorems 4.6 and 5.1]. Berger–Moerdijk [2008.b, Theorem 1.6] and Shulman [2015.b, Theorem 8.9]
replace Γ with more general indexing categories.
Proposition 2.3.3. Suppose W is a combinatorial model category. The category PSh(Γ, W) of W-valued presheaves
on Γ admits a unique generalized Reedy model structure with the following properties:
(1) a natural transformation t: F →G is a weak equivalence if for every c ∈Γ the map tc: F(c) →G(c) is a
weak equivalence in W and a cofibration if for every c ∈Γ the relative latching map
F(c) ⊔LcF LcY →G(c)
is a projective cofibration in WAut(c), where LcF = colimd→c F(d) is the latching object of F at c ∈Γ, given
by the colimit over all nonsurjective maps d →c of finite pointed sets;
(2) the generalized Reedy model structure is combinatorial;
(3) if W is left proper, then so is PSh(Γ, W);
(4) if W is a monoidal model category, then PSh(Γ, W) is a W-enriched monoidal model category.
If W itself is the injective model structure on V-valued presheaves on a small category C, then we refer to the
resulting model structure on
PSh(Γ, PSh(C, V)) ∼= PSh(Γ × C, V)
as the Γ-Reedy injective model structure. Here V is a cartesian combinatorial model category.
Proof. The model structure and its monoidality is established by Lydakis [1999.c, Theorems 4.6 and 5.1] for W = sSet
and Berger–Moerdijk [2008.b, Theorem 7.6 and Example 7.7(b)] for the general case. The generating (acyclic)
cofibrations of PSh(Γ, W) are given by pushout products j □∂c of generating (acyclic) cofibrations j of W and the
boundary inclusion maps ∂c: ∂Yc →Yc, where c ∈Γ and ∂Yc is the subobject of Yc ∈PSh(Γ) such that for every
d ∈Γ the set ∂Yc(d) is the set of noninjective pointed maps of sets c →d.
If W is a monoidal model category, then PSh(Γ, W) is a W-enriched model category because the pushout product
of an (acyclic) cofibration i of W and a generating (acyclic) cofibration j □∂c of PSh(Γ, W) is (i □j) □∂c, where


## Page 14


14
i □j is an (acyclic) cofibration in W by the monoidality of W. By Hovey [1999.b, Corollary 4.2.5], we deduce that
the W-tensoring functor on PSh(Γ, W) is a left Quillen bifunctor. The unit axiom for enrichments follows from the
unit axiom for W.
□
Proposition 2.3.4. Suppose W is a combinatorial model category. The identity functors yield Quillen equivalences
PSh(Γ, W)proj ⇄PSh(Γ, W)Reedy ⇄PSh(Γ, W)inj
of the projective, generalized Reedy (Proposition 2.3.3), and injective model structures on Γ-objects. In particular,
given a cofibrant object X ∈W and ⟨m⟩∈Γ, the object X ⊗Y⟨m⟩is cofibrant in the generalized Reedy model
structure.
Proof. It suffices to show that generating projective cofibrations are generalized Reedy cofibrations and generating
generalized Reedy cofibrations are injective cofibrations. Suppose j: X →Y is a generating cofibration in W and
⟨m⟩∈Γ.
Given a generating projective cofibration j ⊗Y⟨m⟩, we have to show that it is a generalized Reedy cofibration,
i.e., for every c ∈Γ the latching map j □(LcY⟨m⟩→Y⟨m⟩(c)) is a projective cofibration in WAut(c). Following
Lydakis [1999.c, Proposition 3.2], the map
LcY⟨m⟩→Y⟨m⟩(c)
splits as a coproduct injection map
LcY⟨m⟩→LcY⟨m⟩⊔Surj(⟨m⟩, c),
where Surj(⟨m⟩, c) is the set of surjective maps ⟨m⟩→c of pointed sets. Therefore, the latching map is isomorphic
to
idY ⊗LcY⟨m⟩⊔j ⊗Surj(⟨m⟩, c),
which is a cobase change of the map j ⊗Surj(⟨m⟩, c).
The Aut(c)-action on Surj(⟨m⟩, c) is free, so the map
j ⊗Surj(⟨m⟩, c) is a projective cofibration in WAut(c).
Given a generating generalized Reedy cofibration j □∂⟨m⟩, we have to show that it is an injective cofibration,
i.e., after evaluating at an object c ∈Γ we get a cofibration in W. The map ∂⟨m⟩(c) splits as
∂⟨m⟩(c): ∂Y⟨m⟩(c) →∂Y⟨m⟩(c) ⊔Inj(⟨m⟩, c),
where Inj(⟨m⟩, c) denotes the set of injective pointed maps ⟨m⟩→c. This, the map (j □∂⟨m⟩)(c) is isomorphic to
idY ⊗∂Y⟨m⟩(c) ⊔j ⊗Inj(⟨m⟩, c),
which is a cobase change of the cofibration j ⊗Inj(⟨m⟩, c).
□
Definition 2.3.5. Let C and D be small categories and V a symmetric monoidal category (e.g., V = sSet). We
define an external product functor ⊠as follows:
⊠: PSh(C, V) × PSh(D, V) →PSh(C × D, V),
(F ⊠G)(c, d) = F(c) ⊗G(d).
Proposition 2.3.6. Let C, C′, and D be small categories and let V be a combinatorial monoidal model category.
Equip the category of simplicial presheaves on D with the injective model structure. Suppose that one of the
following holds:
(1) the categories of V-valued presheaves on C and C × D are equipped with the injective model structure
(Proposition 2.3.1);
(2) C = Γ × C′ and the categories of V-valued presheaves on C and C × D are equipped with the Γ-Reedy
injective model structure (Proposition 2.3.3).
Then the external product functor of Definition 2.3.5 is a V-enriched left Quillen bifunctor.
Proof. We first prove the claim for Condition (1). We have
(F ⊠G)(c, d) = F(c) ⊗G(d).
The monoidal product F(c) ⊗G(d) is separately V-enriched cocontinuous in F and G. Injective cofibrations and
acyclic cofibrations are defined objectwise. Consequently, the pushout product axioms required for a left Quillen
bifunctor follow from those of the monoidal model category V.
For Condition (2), denote by □the pushout product associated with ⊠. By Proposition 2.3.3, generating (acyclic)
cofibrations for the Γ-Reedy injective model structure on PSh(Γ × C′, V) have the form j □∂c, where c ∈Γ and j is
an (acyclic) injective cofibration in PSh(C′, V). The pushout product of j□∂c and an (acyclic) injective cofibration k
in PSh(D, V) is (j □∂c) □k ∼= (j □k) □∂c. The map j □k is an injective cofibration in PSh(C′ × D, V) by the
first part. Therefore, the map (j □k) □∂c is an (acyclic) cofibration in the Γ-Reedy injective model structure on
PSh(Γ × C′ × D, V).
□


## Page 15


15
We will realize the (∞, 1)-category of sheaves of symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories via a particular model
structure, following Barwick [2005.a, Section 3.1] for (∞, d)-categories and Toën [2000.b, Section 2.2] for symmetric
monoidal (∞, d)-categories. We begin by taking the injective model structure on PSh∆(GCart × Γ × ∆×d). To
encode the Segal conditions for ∆and Γ, completeness and globularity conditions for ∆, and descent conditions for
GCart, we perform a left Bousfield localization of this model category.
Definition 2.3.7. (Hirschhorn [2002.b, Definition 3.1.1.(1)], Barwick [2007, Definition 4.2], Lurie [2017.a, §A.3.7].)
Suppose M is a model category and S is a set of morphisms in M. The left Bousfield localization of M at S is
a model category LSM equipped with a left Quillen functor F: M →LSM that satisfies the following universal
property.
• For any model category N, composition with F induces a bijection between left Quillen functors LSM →N
and left Quillen functors G: M →N such that the left derived functor of G sends elements of S to weak
equivalences in N.
An object X ∈M is S-local if for every morphism f: Y →Z in S, the induced map map(Z, X) →map(Y, X) is
a weak equivalence. A morphism f: Y →Z is an S-local equivalence if, for every S-local object X, the induced
map map(Z, X) →map(Y, X) is a weak equivalence. Here map(−, −) denotes the homotopy function complex in a
model category, as defined in Hirschhorn [2002.b, Definition 17.4.1].
Proposition 2.3.8. (Smith; Barwick [2007, Theorem 4.7].)
Suppose M is a left proper combinatorial model
category and S is a set of morphisms in M. Then the left Bousfield localization (Definition 2.3.7) of M at S exists
and is a left proper combinatorial model category. Moreover, the localization has the following properties:
(1) its underlying category is M;
(2) its weak equivalences are precisely the S-local equivalences;
(3) its cofibrations and acyclic fibrations coincide with those of M;
(4) its fibrant objects are precisely the S-local fibrant objects of M.
Definition 2.3.9. (Barwick [2007, Definition 4.42].) Suppose V is a monoidal model category, M is a V-enriched
model category and S is a set of morphisms in M. The enriched left Bousfield localization of M at S is a V-enriched
model category LSM equipped with a V-enriched left Quillen functor F: M →LSM that satisfies the following
universal property.
• For any V-enriched model category N, composition with F induces a bijection between V-enriched left
Quillen functors LSM →N and V-enriched left Quillen functors G: M →N such that the left derived
functor of G sends elements of S to weak equivalences in N.
An object X ∈M is enriched S-local if for every morphism f: Y →Z in S, the induced map R Map(Z, X) →
R Map(Y, X) is a weak equivalence. A morphism f: Y →Z is an enriched S-local equivalence if, for every S-local
object X, the induced map R Map(Z, X) →R Map(Y, X) is a weak equivalence. Here R Map(−, −) denotes the
derived enriched mapping object in a V-enriched model category.
We will need the following characterization and existence theorem for enriched left Bousfield localizations. Al-
though all of the ideas already appear in the works of Barwick and Gorchinskiy–Guletski˘ı, we provide a proof
because this exact variant of the statement does not appear in either source. (Our version does not assume M to
be tractable and gives a formula for S′ in the enriched case.)
Proposition 2.3.10. (Barwick [2007, Theorem 4.46 and Proposition 4.47], Gorchinskiy–Guletski˘ı [2009.d, Lemma
31 and Remark 32 (journal), Lemma 28 and Remark 29 (arXiv v4)].) Suppose V is a tractable monoidal model
category, M is a left proper combinatorial V-enriched model category, and S is a set of morphisms in M. Then
the V-enriched left Bousfield localization (Definition 2.3.9) of M at S exists and is a left proper combinatorial
V-enriched model category. It can be computed as LS′M, where S′ = {X ⊗L f | X ∈G, f ∈S} and G is a set
of homotopy generators of V, that is, every object of V is weakly equivalent to the homotopy colimit of a small
diagram of objects in G. If LSM is a V-enriched model category, then LSM = LS′M. If M is tractable, then so is
its localization. Moreover, the localization has the following properties:
(1) its underlying V-enriched category is M;
(2) its weak equivalences are precisely the enriched S-local equivalences;
(3) its cofibrations and acyclic fibrations coincide with those of M;
(4) its fibrant objects are precisely the enriched S-local fibrant objects of M.
Proof. Recall that every tractable model category V admits a set G of homotopy generators, which we take (without
loss of generality) to consist of cofibrant objects. For example, we can take G to comprise the domains and codomains
of a set of generating cofibrations with cofibrant domains. Indeed, every object in V admits a cofibrant replacement X


## Page 16


16
such that ∅→X is a transfinite composition of cobase changes of generating cofibrations with cofibrant domains.
Since all objects in the diagram are cofibrant, all pushout squares are homotopy pushout squares. Therefore, the
transfinite composition is a homotopy transfinite composition. Thus, X is a homotopy colimit of a diagram in G.
To show that LS′M is the desired V-enriched left Bousfield localization, we verify that LS′M is a V-enriched
model category, i.e., the tensoring functor V × M →M is a left Quillen bifunctor and the unit axiom is satisfied.
These properties follow from the same properties of M, with the exception of the following property. Suppose
f: X →Y is a generating cofibration with cofibrant domain of V and s: A →B is an acyclic cofibration in LS′M.
Then f □s is a weak equivalence in LS′M. To this end, consider the diagram
X ⊗A
X⊗s


X ⊗B

f⊗B

f□s
#
Y ⊗A
Y ⊗s
/
;
Y ⊗B.
Without loss of generality (performing cofibrant replacements on elements of S as necessary), assume that S
(and therefore also S′ = {X ⊗f}) consists of cofibrations with cofibrant domains. For every s ∈S the functor
−⊗s: V →M →is a left Quillen functor, where M →is equipped with the projective model structure. Therefore,
its left derived functor −⊗L s preserves homotopy colimits. By assumption, −⊗L s sends elements of G to weak
equivalences in LS′M. Since G is a set of homotopy generators of V, the functor −⊗L s sends all objects of V to
weak equivalences in LS′M. Thus, since X and Y are cofibrant, the maps X ⊗s and Y ⊗s are weak equivalences
in LS′M, and so is the cobase change Y ⊗A →P of X ⊗s in the diagram. By the 2-out-of-3 property, the map
f □s is a weak equivalence in LS′M.
To establish the universal property of enriched left Bousfield localizations in Definition 2.3.9, suppose H: M →N
is a V-enriched left Quillen functor into a V-enriched model category N such that the left derived functor of H
sends elements of S to weak equivalences in N. We want to show that H ◦F: LS′M →N is a V-enriched left
Quillen functor. Thanks to our cofibrancy assumptions on G, S, and S′, all functors and tensor products below
are automatically derived. By the universal property of left Bousfield localizations (Definition 2.3.7), it suffices to
show that L(H ◦F) sends elements of S′ to weak equivalences in N. Given s ∈S′, i.e., s = X ⊗f for X ∈G and
f ∈S, we compute L(H ◦F)(s) = H(s) = H(X ⊗f) ∼= X ⊗H(f), where the last isomorphism is induced by the
V-enrichment of H. Since f is a cofibration with cofibrant domain, so is H(f) = LH(f). The latter map is a weak
equivalence by assumption. Since X is cofibrant, X ⊗H(f) computes the derived tensor product, hence is a weak
equivalence, as desired.
If LSM is a V-enriched model category, then taking N = LSM yields a left Quillen functor LS′M →LSM. Since
S ⊂S′, this shows LS′M = LSM.
The characterizations of weak equivalences and fibrant objects follow from their nonenriched versions using the
fact that G is a set of homotopy generators of V.
□
Proposition 2.3.11. Suppose V is a tractable monoidal model category, M is a left proper combinatorial V-enriched
model category, and S is a set of morphisms in M.
We have the following.
(1) If {1V} is a set of homotopy generators of V, then LSM is the V-enriched localization of M at S.
(2) If M is simplicial, then so is LSM.
(3) If M is C∞sSetsing,inj-enriched (Proposition 2.2.5), then so is LSM.
(4) If C is a small category and G is a set of homotopy generators of V, then {X ⊗Yc | X ∈G, c ∈C} is a set
of homotopy generators for M = PSh(C, V) equipped with a model structure whose weak equivalences are
defined objectwise.
Proof. For Part (1), observe that for every f ∈S the map 1V ⊗L f = Q1V ⊗f is weakly equivalent to f by the
unit axiom for V-enriched model structures, hence is a weak equivalence in LSM. Thus, by Proposition 2.3.10, the
model category LSM is V-enriched.
Every simplicial set is a homotopy colimit of a constant point-valued diagram over its category of elements, so
the terminal simplicial set is a homotopy generator of sSet. Thus, Part (1) implies Part (2).
Since C∞sSet is Quillen equivalent to sSet (Proposition 2.2.5), Part (2) implies Part (3).
Part (4) is implied by the enriched co-Yoneda lemma and the fact that homotopy colimits in categories of
presheaves are computed objectwise.
□

Proposition 2.3.19. The model structure PSh∆(S)local on the category of simplicial presheaves on a site S given
by the left Bousfield localization of the injective model structure on PSh∆(S) with respect to the morphisms (2.3.17)
is a left proper combinatorial simplicial cartesian model category.
Proof. The model structure exists and has the desired properties by Propositions 2.3.10 and 2.3.11. For the cartesian
property, observe that the Čech fibrant replacement functor, regarded as an endofunctor on the (nonlocalized)
injective model structure, preserves finite homotopy limits. (See, for example, Lurie [2017.a, Proposition 6.2.2.7].)
Furthermore, it sends Čech maps (2.3.17) to objectwise weak equivalences. Therefore, the homotopy product (in
the nonlocalized injective model structure) of any presheaf with a Čech map (2.3.17) of the form
iU,V : cU →YV
is mapped by the Čech fibrant replacement functor to an objectwise weak equivalence. This means the homotopy
product is itself a Čech-local weak equivalence, as desired.
□


## Page 18

Proposition 2.3.20. (Lydakis [1999.c, Theorems 4.6 and 5.1], Berger–Moerdijk [2008.b, Theorem 1.6], Shulman
[2015.b, Theorem 8.9].) The model structure PSh∆(Γ)local on Γ-spaces given by the left Bousfield localization of
the generalized Reedy model structure on PSh∆(Γ) (Proposition 2.3.3) with respect to the morphisms (2.3.15) and
(2.3.16) is a left proper combinatorial simplicial monoidal model category.
Proof. The model structure exists and has the desired properties by Propositions 2.3.10 and 2.3.11. For the monoidal
property, we need to show that the derived smash product of an arbitrary presheaf and a localizing map is a local
weak equivalence. Observe that the smash product of ⟨r⟩∈Γ with a map (2.3.15)
tκ,ℓ: Y⟨κ⟩⊔Y⟨ℓ⟩→Y⟨κ+ℓ⟩
is again a map of the same form, namely, t⟨κ⟩∧⟨r⟩,⟨ℓ⟩∧⟨r⟩. Likewise, the smash product of τ: ∅→Y⟨0⟩(2.3.16) with
any ⟨r⟩∈Γ is again τ. Both smash products are derived because coproducts of representable objects are projectively
cofibrant, hence generalized Reedy cofibrant.
□
Remark 2.3.21. Since the injective model structure is not monoidal (Remark 2.3.2), neither is the localized in-
jective structure. The injective model structure on Γ-spaces has more cofibrations than the Bousfield–Friedlander
strict model structure [1978, Theorem 3.5], which coincides with the generalized Reedy model structure of Propo-
sition 2.3.3. The generalized Reedy model structure, in turn, has more cofibrations than Schwede’s strict Q-model
structure [1999.d, Lemma 1.4], which coincides with the projective model structure. All three model structures have
the same weak equivalences. The localized model structure on Γ-spaces of Proposition 2.3.20 has fewer weak equiv-
alences than the Bousfield–Friedlander stable model structure [1978, Theorem 5.2] and Schwede’s stable Q-model
structure [1999.d, Theorem 1.5], since we do not impose the group-like property on Γ-spaces.
Definition 2.3.22. The Rezk model structure [1998.b, Theorem 7.2] on ∆-spaces is the left Bousfield localization
of the Reedy model structure on PSh∆(∆) (which coincides with the injective model structure) with respect to the
morphisms (2.3.13) and (2.3.14). We denote this model category by PSh∆(∆)local.
3. Geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories
Notation 3.0.1. Throughout this section, S denotes an arbitrary small site, V denotes an arbitrary combinatorial
cartesian model category, and d is a nonnegative integer denoting the category number, i.e., d in (∞, d)-categories.
In subsequent sections, S will be taken to be a structured cartesian site GCart of Definition 4.1.12 and V will be
taken to be sSet or C∞sSet (Proposition 2.2.5).
3.1. Geometric multiple and globular (∞, d)-categories. To combine symmetric monoidal, d-categorical, and
geometric structures, we lift the morphisms in Definition 2.3.12 to the category of presheaves on the product category
S ×Γ×∆×d, using the external product ⊠of Definition 2.3.5. The resulting model category has fibrant objects that
are local with respect to each morphism f in Definition 2.3.12 after evaluation on arbitrary objects in the factors
not corresponding to f. For instance, evaluation at an object of Γ × ∆×d yields an ∞-sheaf of simplicial sets on S,
which means the homotopy descent condition (2.3.18) is satisfied.
Notation 3.1.1. Assume Notation 3.0.1.
Recall Definition 2.3.12.
We consider the following sets of maps in
PSh(S × Γ × ∆×d, V).
• Let S∆= {Yc ⊠ϕa,b, Yc ⊠x | c ∈S × Γ × ∆{1,...,k−1,k+1,...,d}, 1 ≤k ≤d, a, b ∈∆}, where ϕa,b are the
morphisms (2.3.13) and x is the morphism (2.3.14).
• Let SΓ = {Yc ⊠tκ,ℓ, Yc ⊠τ | c ∈S × ∆×d, κ, ℓ∈Γ}, where tκ,ℓare the morphisms (2.3.15) and τ is the
morphism (2.3.16).
• Let SS = {Yc ⊠iU,V | c ∈Γ × ∆×d, V ∈S}, where iU,V are the morphisms (2.3.17) for a covering family U
of V .

Both types of 1-morphisms can be composed, and 2-cells are squares involving two vertical and two horizontal

Remark 3.1.10. We emphasize that Definition 3.1.8.(1) does not require geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-
categories to be fibrant in the model category GCat⊗
∞,d, or any other model structure with the same weak equiva-
lences. Indeed, all the constructions on geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories considered in the present
work respect weak equivalences (for instance, by being derived). Hence, performing a fibrant replacement of the
bordism category will only alter the constructions up to weak equivalence.
A useful analogy is provided by the notion of a derived category in homological algebra. An object of the derived
category of a ring R is a chain complex of R-modules. A morphism in the derived category can be presented as
a composition of a chain map and a formal inverse of a quasi-isomorphism. This formal inverse can be viewed as
a (fibrant) resolution of the target. Requiring all objects in the derived category to be injectively fibrant chain
complexes yields an equivalent derived category in which all morphisms can now be presented as chain maps.
However, this approach is not commonly adopted in homological algebra, where chain complexes that are neither
injectively fibrant nor projectively cofibrant are considered valid objects in the derived category, on par with
injectively fibrant and projectively cofibrant objects.
From a practical standpoint, requiring bordism categories to be fibrant does not result in tangible benefits, as
fibrant objects have good properties regarding morphisms into them, whereas a functorial field theory is a morphism
originating from a bordism category. Owing to our choice of the injective model structure, all objects of GCat⊗
∞,d
are cofibrant. In particular, all bordism categories introduced in this paper are cofibrant, which eliminates the need
for additional cofibrant replacements when discussing functorial field theories.
3.2. (∞, d)-categories of functors. In this section, we define an object Fun⊗(X, Y ) that encodes the geomet-
ric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category of functors between geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories X
and Y . The model categories in Propositions 3.1.2 and 3.1.7 cannot be made into monoidal model categories in a
straightforward way, so constructing this mapping object is somewhat subtle. We remark that it is not necessary
to understand the details of the construction in this section to use the abstract properties of the mapping object
(Proposition 3.2.10).
Definition 3.2.1. Assume Notation 3.0.1. We equip the category
PSh(S × Γ × ∆×d, V) = PSh(Γ, PSh(S × ∆×d, V))
with a monoidal structure given by the Day convolution (Notation 2.2.1) induced by the smash product on Γ and
the categorical product on PSh(S × ∆×d, V).
The uple and globular model categories
PSh(S × Γ × ∆×d, V)uple,
PSh(S × Γ × ∆×d, V)glob
(Propositions 3.1.2 and 3.1.7) are not monoidal model categories (Remark 2.3.2 and Example 3.2.7).
In the uple case, the problem is caused by the fact that the Day convolution monoidal product quotients by
the group of automorphisms of objects in Γ (Remark 2.3.2). This problem is resolved by passing to the Γ-Reedy
injective model structure (Proposition 2.3.3).
In the globular case, there is one more problem: the globular weak equivalences are not closed under cartesian
products with objects of ∆×d (Example 3.2.7). This problem is fixed by passing from ∆×d to Joyal’s category Θd
[1997], which yields a Quillen equivalent monoidal model structure (Proposition 3.2.8). The monoidal product
and internal hom can then be transferred from the resulting monoidal model category via the Quillen equivalence
(Definition 3.2.9).

2-morphisms. The restriction map Map(Y[1],[1], T) →Map(Y[1],[0], T) is not a weak equivalence.

3.3. Categorical operations in geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories. The main purpose of
this section is to demonstrate the explicit construction of categorical operations such as composition of morphisms
and monoidal products of objects in a fixed (∞, d)-category T (Definition 3.1.8.(2)). Readers uninterested in such
explicit constructions may skip this section.
Notation 3.3.1. In this section, we maintain the conventions of Notation 3.0.1 and assume T is a fibrant object in
the model category GCat⊗
∞,d (Definition 3.1.8.(2)). All results presented in this section also apply equally if T is a
fibrant object in GCat⊗
∞,d (Definition 3.1.8.(1)), provided that references to isotopies are disregarded. Without loss
of generality, we also impose the fibrancy condition of Remark 3.3.10. Additionally, U denotes an arbitrary object
of the site S.
Remark 3.3.2. If T is a nonfibrant object, the operations constructed in this section can be performed, provided
the results are interpreted within the fibrant replacement. Indeed, by the factorization axioms of a model category,
the map T →RT into the fibrant replacement is an acyclic cofibration, hence a monomorphism. This implies that
data in T can be regarded as residing in the fibrant replacement. Hence, the constructions below can be applied
by replacing T with its fibrant replacement RT and composing the input map I →T (from (3.3.4)) with the map
T →RT.
The following proposition will be employed in the subsequent constructions.
Proposition 3.3.3. Recall from Notation 2.2.1 the enriched mapping object functor
Map(−, −): (GCat⊗
∞,d)op × GCat⊗
∞,d →C∞sSet.
Suppose T is a fibrant object in the model category GCat⊗
∞,d (Definition 3.1.8.(2)) and w: I →A is an acyclic
cofibration in GCat⊗
∞,d. Then the induced morphism
Map(w, T): Map(A, T) →Map(I, T)
is an acyclic fibration between fibrant objects. Furthermore, the fiber of Map(w, T) at every point of Map(I, T) is
a contractible fibrant object.
Proof. All objects in GCat⊗
∞,d are cofibrant, the object T is fibrant by assumption, the map w is an acyclic cofibration
by assumption, and the functor Map(−, −) is a right Quillen functor. Therefore, the morphism Map(w, T) is an
acyclic fibration between fibrant objects. In particular, Map computes the derived mapping objects. The terminal
object 1 is fibrant in GCat⊗
∞,d. Therefore, for every map x: 1 →Map(I, T), the pullback P of Map(w, T) along x is
an acyclic fibrant object, which implies P is contractible.
□
The typical usage of Proposition 3.3.3 is as follows. We aim to perform a categorical operation on input data,
which is encoded as a morphism
(3.3.4)
in: I →T.
The output of the operation is unique up to a contractible space of choices. Moreover, there are typically coherences
that demonstrate a specific output to be the result of applying an operation to the input data. For example, the
composition of 1-morphisms is represented by a 2-simplex. We encode the input data, output data, and auxiliary
data (tracking coherences) as a morphism
(3.3.5)
aux: A →T.
There is an inclusion map w: I →A, which expresses the fact that A contains information about the input data.
We require this inclusion to be a weak equivalence, hence an acyclic cofibration, which implies that the fiber P in
the proof of Proposition 3.3.3 is contractible. In other words, the space of ways to extend the input data to output
data is a contractible space P. Finally, we extract the desired output by restricting along a map O →A, which is
not necessarily an acyclic cofibration. This produces a morphism
(3.3.6)
out: O →T.

where, on the right, we have applied currying to incorporate the domain of the value category of presheaves

Definition 3.3.8. Assume Notation 3.3.1. We define the following categorical structures.
• A U-family of objects in T is a morphism in GCat⊗
∞,d
x: objU := Y(U,⟨1⟩,[0],...,[0]),R0,∆0 →T,
• Let 1 ≤k ≤d. A U-family of k-morphisms in T is a morphism
fk: mork,U := Y(U,⟨1⟩,[1],...,[1],[0],...,[0]),R0,∆0 →T,
with k copies of [1]. If k = 1, we write morU = mor1,U and call f = f1 a morphism.
• A U-family of virtual isomorphisms in T is a morphism
v: virU := Y(U,⟨1⟩,[0],...,[0]),R0,∆1 →T.
• An isotopy of U-families of objects in T from an object x to an object y is a morphism
i: isotU := Y(U,⟨1⟩,[0],...,[0]),R1,∆0 →T,
whose restrictions along the maps objU →isotU, which are induced by the inclusions R0 ∼= {s} ⊂R1 and
R0 ∼= {t} ⊂R1 (in the C∞Cart factor), coincide with x and y, respectively. Here s, t ∈R are distinct real
numbers.
• A composed pair of U-families of 1-morphisms is a map
c: ∆-morU := Y(U,⟨1⟩,[2],[0],··· ,[0]),R0,∆0 →T.
• A multiplied pair of U-families of objects in T is a morphism
m: mobjU := Y(U,⟨2⟩,[0],...,[0]),R0,∆0 →T.
• In general, a multiplied pair of categorical structures is obtained by replacing ⟨1⟩with ⟨2⟩. For example, a
multiplied pair of U-families of isotopies is a morphism
m: misotU := Y(U,⟨2⟩,[0],...,[0]),R1,∆0 →T.
• An empty tuple of U-families of objects is a map
∅U := Y(U,⟨0⟩,[0],...,[0]),R0,∆0 →T.
See Remark 3.3.10 for an explanation of empty tuples.
• An empty tuple of isotopies of U-families of objects in T is a morphism
∅isotU := Y(U,⟨0⟩,[0],...,[0]),R1,∆0 →T.


## Page 26

Remark 3.3.9. The definitions of virtual isomorphism and isotopy require some explanation. Recall that in the
Segal space formalism, there are (a priori) two distinct notions of isomorphism: a 1-simplex in the space of objects,
or a vertex in the space of morphisms whose image in the homotopy category is an isomorphism.
We call an
isomorphism of the first type a virtual isomorphism and an isomorphism of the second type an isomorphism. For
Segal complete objects, these two notions of isomorphism are equivalent: the simplicial degeneration map from the
moduli space of virtual isomorphisms to the moduli space of isomorphisms is a weak equivalence.
For geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories with isotopies, the isotopies play a similar role to virtual
isomorphisms. It is possible to combine both virtual isomorphisms and isotopies into a single simplicial object,
using the Quillen equivalence between C∞sSet and sSet (Proposition 2.2.5). However, we find that it is useful to
keep track of this data separately in practice. Virtual isomorphisms keep track of categorical data encoded by the
fields (i.e., gauge transformations), while isotopies keep track of smooth isotopies of such data.
Remark 3.3.10. To simplify the presentation below, we impose one more condition on the (∞, d)-category T:
evaluating T at an object whose Γ-component is ⟨0⟩∈Γ yields the terminal object in V.
This condition is
already satisfied for our variant of the bordism category.
In a generic setting, it does not result in a loss of
generality: for every T we can construct a weak equivalence T →T ′, where T ′ satisfies the additional property.
Indeed, take T →T ′ to be the fibrant replacement map of T in the model structure given by enlarging the class
of cofibrations in Definition 3.1.8.(1) to include maps given by tensoring the representable presheaf of an object
(U, ⟨0⟩, m) ∈S × Γ × ∆×d with an arbitrary map in V. The right lifting property with respect to the latter maps
guarantees that evaluating T on ⟨0⟩∈Γ yields the terminal object.
The advantage of this additional condition can be explained as follows. Given a pair of entities like in Defini-
tion 3.3.8, specified as maps out of representable presheaves on objects with Γ-component ⟨1⟩∈Γ, e.g.,
x, y: objU →T,
we would like to multiply them by lifting the map
[x, y]: objU ⊔objU →T
along the map
λ: Y⟨1⟩⊔Y⟨1⟩→Y⟨2⟩
and restricting along the map Y⟨1⟩→Y⟨2⟩, as explained in Example 3.3.11. The problem with the first step is that
the map λ is not a monomorphism: the two elements corresponding to the maps of finite pointed sets ⟨1⟩→⟨0⟩in
the first and second summands both map to the same element in Y⟨2⟩.
The underlying meaning of this fact is that restricting along the map Y⟨0⟩→Y⟨1⟩amounts to discarding the only
element in a 1-tuple. After restriction, we get a map out of Y⟨0⟩, which encodes an (empty) 0-tuple. The fibrancy
conditions for Γ-spaces force the space of 0-tuples to be contractible. However, there is no reason why two 0-tuples
arising from two different objects should be equal. Enforcing the additional condition on T makes all 0-tuples equal.
This makes the noninjective map
ρ: Y⟨1⟩⊔Y⟨1⟩→Y⟨1⟩⊔Y⟨0⟩Y⟨1⟩
satisfy the unique left lifting property against the object T, so any map out of Y⟨1⟩⊔Y⟨1⟩can be canonically extended
along the map ρ, e.g., the map [x, y] lifts uniquely to a map
[x, y]∗: objU ⊔∅U objU →T.
We will systematically use the notation [x, y]∗for such lifts.
Given the preceding definitions, we can explicitly construct the composition of morphisms and monoidal products
of objects in T. We begin with the monoidal product.

4. Geometric sites and fibered geometric sites
In this section, we introduce the notion of a geometric site and a fibered geometric site. These notions allow us
to generalize smooth families of bordisms to more structured types of parametrized bordisms, such as superfamilies
of bordisms or holomorphic families. This generality allows us to define the geometric bordism category. In the
special case where parametrizing families are smooth manifolds, we obtain the smooth bordism category.
4.1. Geometric sites. References on admissibility structures and geometric sites include Joyal–Moerdijk [1994.a],
Dubuc [2000.a], Carchedi [2013.c], Lurie [2018.b, Chapter 20], Clough [2023, Section 2].
Definition 4.1.1. An admissibility structure on a category C is a subcategory Cad ⊂C that contains all identity
morphisms of C and satisfies the following properties.
(1) Admissible morphisms are closed under retracts in the category of morphisms in C.
(2) If morphisms g and g ◦f are admissible, then so is f.
(3) Base changes of admissible morphisms along arbitrary morphisms in C exist. Furthermore, such base changes
are themselves admissible morphisms.
We have the following definitions.
A geometric category is a category equipped with an admissibility structure. A geometric site is a site equipped
with an admissibility structure such that every covering family consists of admissible morphisms.
We have a similar definition in the C∞Set-enriched case, given by imposing the relevant structure on the under-
lying category, obtained by evaluation at R0 ∈C∞Cart.
Definition 4.1.2. An C∞Set-enriched admissibility structure on a C∞Set-enriched category C is a C∞Set-enriched
subcategory Cad ⊂C such that for every L ∈C∞Cart, the inclusion Cad
L ⊂CL (Notation 2.2.2) satisfies Conditions (1)
and (2) of Definition 4.1.1 and for L = R0 also Condition (3).
A C∞Set-enriched geometric category is a C∞Set-enriched category equipped with a C∞Set-enriched admissibility
structure. A C∞Set-enriched geometric site is a C∞Set-enriched site (Definition 2.1.6) equipped with a C∞Set-
enriched admissibility structure such that every covering family consists of admissible morphisms.
Remark 4.1.3. For a C∞Set-enriched admissibility structure (Definition 4.1.2), Condition (3) of Definition 4.1.1
is often violated when L ̸= R0. Consider the setting of Definition 4.2.12: C∞Embd is the C∞Set-enriched site of
smooth families of d-dimensional smooth manifolds, with mapping objects encoding smooth families of fiberwise
smooth open embeddings. Consider objects p1, p2, q ∈C∞Embd and L-families of open embeddings fi: pi →q in
C∞Embd. Suppose that for different points l ∈L the pullbacks of (f1)l and (f2)l in C∞Embd (Definition 4.2.1) are
not isomorphic as objects in C∞Embd. Then there is no pullback of f1 and f2 in C∞Embd.
Definition 4.1.4. The sites C∞Man and C∞Cart (Definition 2.1.8) are turned into geometric sites (Definition 4.1.1)
by taking open embeddings as admissible morphisms.


## Page 31


31
We now recall some elementary facts about Grothendieck fibrations, which will be needed in this section. Recall
that a Grothendieck fibration f: C →D is a functor such that for all y ∈C and morphisms b: z →f(y) ∈D, there is
an f-cartesian morphism a: x →y such that f(a) = b. A choice of an f-cartesian lifting a: x →y for each y and b is
called a cleavage. A cleavage is called a splitting cleavage if it is compatible with compositions and identity maps.
A Grothendieck fibration admitting a splitting cleavage is called split. A Grothendieck fibration is called discrete if
the lifting a is unique (and therefore necessarily f-cartesian), hence there is a canonical splitting cleavage.
A Grothendieck fibration f with a cleavage corresponds to a pseudofunctor Dop →Cat, which sends d ∈D 7→
f −1(d). The structure maps for the pseudofunctor are defined using f-cartesian liftings and depend on a choice of
cleavage. If f admits a splitting cleavage, then the corresponding functor is strict. If the fibration is discrete, the
corresponding functor takes values in discrete categories. The inverse construction is the Grothendieck construction
∫: Fun(Dop, Cat) →Cat/D.
Given a functor Dop →Cat that takes values in discrete small categories, i.e., in sets, applying the functor ∫yields
a discrete fibration. The domain of the corresponding fibration is just the category of elements of the functor.
Definition 4.1.5. Suppose C and D are C∞Set-enriched categories. A C∞Set-enriched Grothendieck fibration is
a C∞Set-enriched functor f: C →D such that for every L ∈C∞Cart the functor fL: CL →DL (Notation 2.2.2) is a
Grothendieck fibration and for every morphism L →L′ the structure map fL′ →fL is a morphism of Grothendieck
fibrations, i.e., preserves cartesian arrows. If C or D is an ordinary category, we promote it to a C∞Set-enriched
category using the inclusion Set →C∞Set.
A C∞Set-enriched cleavage of f is given by a choice of cleavage for every fL that is natural in L ∈C∞Cart. Such
a cleavage is splitting if for every L ∈C∞Cart the cleavage of fL is splitting.
Definition 4.1.6. Let C be a category and let D be a geometric category (Definition 4.1.1). A functor f: C →D
is called an admissibility fibration if the following condition holds.
• Given an object y ∈C and an admissible map b: z →fy there is an f-cartesian arrow a: x →y such that
fa = b.
Similarly, if C is a C∞Set-enriched category and D is a C∞Set-enriched geometric category (Definition 4.1.2), a
C∞Set-enriched functor f: C →D is called a C∞Set-enriched admissibility fibration if the functor fR0: CR0 →DR0
is an admissibility fibration.
Remark 4.1.7. Suppose f: C →D is an admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6). By Condition (2) of Defini-
tion 4.1.1, a morphism in f −1Dad is cartesian for the Grothendieck fibration f −1Dad →Dad if and only if it is
cartesian for the Grothendieck fibration f: C →D.
Remark 4.1.8. For a C∞Set-enriched admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6) f: C →D, the functor fL: CL →DL
is often not an admissibility fibration when L ̸= R0. Consider the setting of Example 4.2.21: HolEmbd is the C∞Set-
enriched site of holomorphic families of d/2-dimensional holomorphic manifolds, with mapping objects encoding
smooth families of fiberwise holomorphic embeddings. The functor Re: HolEmbd →C∞Embd forgets the holomorphic
structure.
Consider objects p, q ∈HolEmbd and an L-family of fiberwise open embeddings f: Re p →Re q in
(C∞Embd)L. Suppose that for different points l ∈L the pullbacks of the fiberwise holomorphic structure on q along
the maps fl are not biholomorphic. Then there is no cartesian lifting of f in (HolEmbd)L.
Proposition 4.1.9. Suppose C is a category, D is a geometric site (Definition 4.1.1), and f: C →D is an admissi-
bility fibration (Definition 4.1.6). Then C admits the structure of a geometric site where:
(a) a morphism a: x →y in C is admissible if and only if it is f-cartesian and fa is admissible in D;
(b) a family of morphisms {ai: xi →x}i∈I is a covering family if and only if ai is f-cartesian for all i ∈I and
{fai: fxi →fx} is a covering family in D.
Suppose C is a C∞Set-enriched category, D is a C∞Set-enriched geometric site (Definition 4.1.2), and f: C →D
is a C∞Set-enriched admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6). Then C admits the structure of a C∞Set-enriched
geometric site where:
(a) a morphism a: x →y in CL is admissible if and only if it is fL-cartesian and fL(a) is admissible in DL;
(b) a family of morphisms {ai: xi →x}i∈I in CR0 is a covering family if and only if ai is fR0-cartesian for all
i ∈I and {fai: fxi →fx} is a covering family in D.
Proof. Below we give a proof for the unenriched case, which also proves the C∞Set-enriched statement by taking f
to be the functor fL: CL →DL for every L ∈C∞Cart for Conditions (1) and (2) in Definition 4.1.1 and for L = R0
for Condition (3).

We begin by proving that Part (a) indeed defines an admissible structure. Define Cad to be the replete subcategory
whose morphisms are f-cartesian arrows whose image under f is admissible in D. We must prove that Cad satisfies
Conditions (1), (2), and (3) in Definition 4.1.1. To prove Condition (1), let a be a retract of a cartesian arrow b such
that fb is admissible. Since cartesian arrows are closed under retracts, a is cartesian. Since fa is a retract of fb, fa
is admissible. Hence, a is admissible. For Condition (2), let a: x →y, b: y →z be morphisms in C such that b and
ba are cartesian and f(b) and f(ba) are admissible in D. By Johnstone [2002.a, Lemma B1.3.3(i)], a is cartesian.
Since fb and f(ba) are admissible in D, so is fa by Condition (2) of Definition 4.1.1. Thus, a is admissible because
a is f-cartesian and fa is admissible in D. For Condition (3), we proceed as follows.

the resulting object denoted by p and morphisms denoted by φ and γ:
