Remark 4.1.10. If f: C →D is an admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6), we refer to admissible maps in C (i.e.,
f-cartesian arrows with an admissible f-image) using the same term as admissible maps in D. The same convention


## Page 33


33
is used for admissible subobjects, i.e., subobjects defined by an admissible map.
For example, if D = C∞Cart
or D = C∞Man, this yields a notion of an open embedding and open subobject in C. If f is a C∞Set-enriched
admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6) and L ∈C∞Cart, we refer to admissible maps in CL as L-families of
admissible maps in C. For example, if D = C∞Cart or D = C∞Man, this yields the notion of an L-family of open
embeddings in C.
If r is an admissible map in C and f(r) is a monomorphism in D, then r is a monomorphism in C. Indeed, if
h1 and h2 are parallel morphisms in C such that rh1 = rh2, then f(r)f(h1) = f(r)f(h2), hence f(h1) = f(h2)
because f(r) is a monomorphism. By the universal property of the f-cartesian arrow r, we obtain h1 = h2, so r is
a monomorphism.
Likewise, if f: C →D is a C∞Set-enriched admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6) and r is an L-family of
admissible maps in C such that f(r) is a monomorphism in DL, then r is a monomorphism in CL.
Proposition 4.1.11. If f: C →D is an admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6) and every admissible map in D is
a monomorphism, then f establishes a bijection from the poset of admissible subobjects (Remark 4.1.10) of c ∈C
to the poset of admissible subobjects of d = f(c) ∈D.
Proof. If r: c1 →c is an admissible monomorphism in C, then f(r) is an admissible monomorphism in D. Indeed,
f(r) is admissible by Definition 4.1.6, therefore f(r) is a monomorphism by assumption. Conversely, if s: d1 →d
is an admissible monomorphism in D, then every cartesian lift of s is an admissible map r: c1 →c, which is a
monomorphism by Remark 4.1.10. If s1: d1 →d and s2: d2 →d are admissible monomorphisms in D such that s1
factors through s2 and r1: c1 →c and r2: c2 →c are f-cartesian lifts of s1 and s2, respectively, then the universal
property of the f-cartesian arrow r2 shows that r1 factors through r2. Thus, these constructions are order-preserving
and are mutually inverse to each other.
□
Definition 4.1.12. Recall the geometric site C∞Cart from Definition 2.1.8.(2). A structured cartesian site is a pair
(GCart, Re), where GCart is a small category and
Re: GCart →C∞Cart
is an admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6). We equip GCart with the structure of a geometric site given by
Proposition 4.1.9. We refer to the functor Re as the reduction functor and to admissible morphisms in GCart as
open embeddings (Remark 4.1.10).
Remark 4.1.13. By Definition 4.1.12 and Proposition 4.1.11, the reduction functor Re establishes a bijective
order-preserving correspondence between open subobjects of X ∈GCart and open subobjects of Re X ∈C∞Cart.
Example 4.1.14. The site C∞Cart (Definition 2.1.8.(2)) with the identity reduction functor Re = id: C∞Cart →
C∞Cart is a structured cartesian site (Definition 4.1.12).
Example 4.1.15. The inclusion 1 →C∞Cart of the full subcategory 1 of C∞Cart on terminal objects (i.e., cartesian
spaces diffeomorphic to R0) into C∞Cart is a structured cartesian site (Definition 4.1.12). Constructions with this
site yield bordism categories without geometric families.
Many more examples can be constructed for manifolds with locally defined structures using Proposition 4.1.9.
Example 4.1.16. The category HolMan of holomorphic manifolds and holomorphic maps, together with the for-
getful functor Re: HolMan →C∞Man (Definition 2.1.8.(1)) satisfies the conditions of Proposition 4.1.9 and therefore
gives rise to a geometric site HolMan. Indeed, given an object U ∈HolCart and an open embedding i: V →Re(U),
a cartesian lift i∗U →U is given by pulling back the complex structure on U to V along i.
The full subcategory on objects whose underlying smooth manifold is an object of C∞Cart (Definition 2.1.8.(2))
yields a structured cartesian site (Definition 4.1.12) HolCart together with the reduction functor Re: HolCart →
C∞Cart. (Such holomorphic manifolds need not be biholomorphic with Cn, as witnessed by the unit disk in C.)
We now define the geometric site of smooth supermanifolds. There are two variants of smooth supermanifolds:
real and complex. There is a complexification functor from real to complex smooth supermanifolds, which is faithful,
but not full or essentially surjective. It restricts to an equivalence on the full subcategories of reduced supermanifolds,
both of which are equivalent to the category of ordinary smooth manifolds. For details, see Deligne–Morgan [1999.e,
Section 4.8] and the nLab [2026.b]. Below, we define both real and complex smooth supermanifolds, as these variants
are needed in Examples 5.1.12 and 5.1.8.
Definition 4.1.17. Denote by k the field R or C. The geometric site C∞SMank (Definition 4.1.1) is defined as
follows. Objects are real/complex smooth supermanifolds M, defined as pairs (M0, M1), where M0 is a smooth
manifold and M1 is a sheaf on M0 valued in CSAlgk, the category of commutative k-superalgebras, i.e., Z/2-graded

subset and Λk denotes the Z/2-graded exterior k-algebra of a k-module. Morphisms (M0, M1) →(N0, N1) are pairs
(f0, f1), where f0: M0 →N0 is a smooth map and f1: N1 →M1 is an f0-map of sheaves (Stacks Project [2025.b,
Tag 008J]).

Following Definition 2.1.7, the following definition is designed so that for a fixed smooth real/complex super-
manifold N, the class of smooth supermanifolds M that admit a closed inclusion into N is a set. Furthermore,
the full subcategory of the slice category C∞SMank/N on included submanifolds is a poset, in particular, every
isomorphism is identity and the map f: M →N can be recovered from M and N.

of C∞SMank (Definition 4.1.17) (with the induced coverage and reduction functor) on objects S such that S is

Definition 4.1.20. The Cahiers site C∞Cahiers (Dubuc [1979]; see also Moerdijk–Reyes [1991, Appendix 2]) is
defined as follows. Objects are pairs (M, W), where M ∈C∞Cart (Definition 2.1.8.(2)) and W is a Weil algebra (Weil

where f is a morphism in C∞Cart and g is a homomorphism of real algebras whose reduction is

We have a reduction functor

4.2. Fibered geometric sites. In this section, we define the notion of a fibered geometric site. Roughly speaking,
a fibered geometric site encodes the data of a structured cartesian family of structured manifolds. For example, in
the case where the geometric structure is complex, we get a holomorphic family of holomorphic manifolds. Looking
ahead, one can regard such a family as providing the data of a holomorphic family of bordisms, equipped with a
holomorphic structure.
Definition 4.2.1. Let d ≥0. Recall the geometric sites C∞Cart and C∞Man from Definition 2.1.8 and Defini-
tion 4.1.4. We define a category C∞Embd and a functor
♭: C∞Embd →C∞Cart
as follows.
• The objects of C∞Embd are morphisms p: M →U in C∞Man such that U ∈C∞Cart and the map p is a
submersion with d-dimensional fibers. We require that p is the restriction of a projection Rm × U →U, for
some m ≥0, to a closed included manifold (Definition 2.1.7) M ⊂Rm × U.

Remark 4.2.2. Definition 4.2.1 requires that the map p is the restriction of a projection of the form Rm ×U →U.
Continuing Remark 2.1.9, this singles out a small subcategory of the large category of submersions. This requirement
is not essential, but it implies that the functor ♭: C∞Embd →C∞Cart is a split Grothendieck fibration. This allows
us to make the bordism category strictly functorial with respect to maps U →V in GCart. We could drop this
requirement, but then the bordism category would only be a pseudofunctor with respect to these maps.
As indicated in Remark 4.2.2, we will show that the base space functor ♭: C∞Embd →C∞Cart is a split Grothen-
dieck fibration.

Next we generalize the site C∞Embd to allow for more types of geometric structures, as in the case of geometric
sites. We make the following definition.
Definition 4.2.4. Fix d ≥0 and recall the site C∞Embd from Definition 4.2.1. A fibered geometric site is a triple
(♭, Ret, Reb), where:
(1) ♭: GEmbd →GCart is a split Grothendieck fibration;
(2) (GCart, Reb) is a structured cartesian site (Definition 4.1.12);
(3) Ret: GEmbd →C∞Embd is an admissibility fibration (Definition 4.1.6);
such that the following conditions hold.
(4) The pair (Ret, Reb) is a morphism of split Grothendieck fibrations, meaning the functor Ret preserves the

commutes (strictly), where ♭: C∞Embd →C∞Cart is the split Grothendieck fibration of Proposition 4.2.3.
(5) The functor ♭: GEmbd →GCart preserves and reflects admissible arrows.
We equip GEmbd with the structure of a geometric site via the admissibility fibration Ret, using Proposition 4.1.9.
When the context is clear, we will omit the subscripts t and b on the functor Re. We refer to the functor Re as
the reduction functor and to admissible morphisms in GEmbd as open embeddings. Thus, an open embedding in
GEmbd is an Re-lift of an open embedding in C∞Embd.
We write q: N →V to indicate that q is an object in the category GEmbd and ♭q = V . The object q gives rise
to a map of manifolds Re q: Re N →Re V , even though N on its own is a purely syntactic construction that does
not denote an object in any category.
Proposition 4.2.5. Let (♭, Ret, Reb) be a fibered geometric site (Definition 4.2.4). Every morphism f: p →q ∈
GEmbd uniquely factors as a Ret-cartesian arrow g: p →p′ such that ♭g = id (hence an open embedding), followed
by a split ♭-cartesian arrow h: p′ →q.
Proof. Let h: p′ →q be the ♭-cartesian lift of ♭f for q. By the universal property of h, there is a unique morphism
g: p →p′ such that hg = f and ♭g = id. But since ♭g = id, Condition (5) of Definition 4.2.4 implies that g is
Ret-cartesian. The uniqueness follows from the universal property of the ♭-cartesian lift h.
□


## Page 37

Proposition 4.2.6. Suppose all conditions of Definition 4.2.4 are satisfied except Condition (5). Assume that
the functor ♭preserves admissible arrows and every arrow f in GEmbd such that ♭f = id is Ret-cartesian. Then ♭
reflects admissible arrows, hence Definition 4.2.4 is satisfied.
Proof. Suppose f is a morphism in GEmbd such that ♭f is admissible in GCart. We have to show that f is admissible
in GEmbd, i.e., Ret f is admissible in C∞Embd and f is Ret-cartesian. Observe that Ret f is admissible in C∞Embd
if and only if ♭Ret f is admissible in C∞Cart. We have ♭Ret f = Reb ♭f, and the latter morphism is admissible
because ♭f is admissible.
To show that f is Ret-cartesian, use the proof of Proposition 4.2.5 to factor f as ζ ◦φ, where φ: p →q is a
split ♭-cartesian arrow and ζ is a morphism in GEmbd such that ♭ζ = id. The morphism ζ is Ret-cartesian because
♭ζ = id.
It remains to show that φ is Ret-cartesian. Consider the corresponding universal problem: given ψ: r →q and
χRe: Ret r →Ret p such that Ret φ ◦χRe = Ret ψ, we have to construct χ: r →p such that φ ◦χ = ψ and Ret χ = χRe.
Construct the map χ♭: ♭r →♭p using the universal property of the Ret-cartesian arrow ♭φ: ♭p →♭q applied to the
maps ♭ψ: ♭r →♭q and ♭χRe: Reb ♭r →Reb ♭p (using Reb ♭= ♭Ret). Take χ: r →p to be the map induced by the
universal property of the ♭-cartesian arrow φ applied to the maps ψ: r →q and χ♭: ♭r →♭p.

Remark 4.2.7. Every open embedding in GEmbd is a monomorphism by Remark 4.1.10 and the fact that open
embeddings in C∞Embd are monomorphisms.
Example 4.2.8. The triple (C∞Embd, id, ♭), where C∞Embd and ♭are as in Definition 4.2.1 is a fibered geometric
site (Definition 4.2.4).

splitting cleavages of ♭L: (GEmbd)L →GCart is natural in L.

♭g = id (hence an open embedding), followed by a split ♭-cartesian arrow h: p′ →q.

5. Geometric bordism categories
In this section, we give a precise definition of the geometric bordism categories as geometric symmetric monoidal
(∞, d)-categories. Specifically, for every field stack F ∈Fieldd (Definition 5.1.1), we define a corresponding geometric
symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category BordF
d (Definition 5.3.7) that encodes bordisms equipped with fields given by F.
The construction is manifestly functorial in F, which proves Axiom (A1). In Section 6, we will show that BordF
d
satisfies the additional Axioms (A2) and (A3) in Definition 1.0.2.
In parallel, we also define a geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category BordF
d
with isotopies (Defini-
tion 5.3.16) that encodes bordisms with isotopies and uses enriched field stacks in Fieldd (Definition 5.1.3). This
proves Axiom (A1) for Bordd.
In Section 6, we will prove that this version of the bordism category satisfies
Axioms (A2) and (A3) in Definition 1.0.5.
We begin by defining the notion of a field stack. In the construction of the bordism category, field stacks will be
used to encode fields on bordisms.
5.1. Field stacks. As pointed out in the introduction, our definition of field stacks generalizes the traditional
notion of tangential structures. Roughly, the passage from the traditional approach to our approach is as follows.
Starting with a tangential structure ξ: Y →BGL(d) and a d-dimensional smooth manifold M (to be thought of as
the ambient manifold of a bordism), we can form the space of sections of the left map in the homotopy pullback
Y ×BGL(d) M
/

Y
ξ

M
τ
/ BGL(d),
where τ: M →BGL(d) is the classifying map for the tangent bundle of M. If we vary the manifold M, we obtain
a sheaf on the site whose objects are d-dimensional manifolds and morphisms are open embeddings. Moreover, we
will need fields to vary in families, parametrized over cartesian spaces. We resolve these issues by working with
sheaves on the site C∞Embd of submersions (with d-dimensional fibers), with fiberwise open embeddings between
them, and its geometric refinement GEmbd.
Throughout this section, we fix an arbitrary natural number d.
Definition 5.1.1. Fix a fibered geometric site GEmbd (Definition 4.2.4). A d-dimensional field stack is a simplicial
presheaf on GEmbd. We equip the category of d-dimensional field stacks
Fieldd := PSh∆(GEmbd)inj,ˇCech
with the cartesian model structure of Proposition 2.3.19.
Remark 5.1.2. Sheaves on the site C∞Embd (Definition 4.2.1) were considered (in an equivalent reformulation)
by Nijenhuis [1958], who used them to define natural bundles and natural mappings in differential geometry.
The latter are closely related to the geometric cobordism hypothesis, as shown in Grady–Pavlov [2021.c]. The
case of nonfiberwise field stacks (using the Quillen equivalent model category of simplicial sheaves on the site of
manifolds and etale maps) was considered by Freed–Teleman [2012, Appendix A]. The special case of field stacks
valued in groupoids instead of simplicial sets is considered by Ludewig–Stoffel [2020.a], where sheaves on the site
of submersions with fiberwise etale maps are considered. By the above remarks, our field stacks are a natural
generalization of these structures. Ayala–Francis–Tanaka [2014.c] investigate the enriched (Definition 5.1.3) and
unenriched variants of presheaves on Embd and Embd (without geometric families).
Definition 5.1.3. Fix a fibered geometric site with isotopies GEmbd (Definition 4.2.13). A d-dimensional isotopy
field stack is an object in the C∞sSet-enriched model category
Fieldd := PSh(GEmbd, C∞sSet)inj,ˇCech,
given by the left Bousfield localization of the injective model structure on PSh(GEmbd, C∞sSet) with respect to
the morphisms (2.3.17). Here PSh denotes the category of C∞sSet-enriched presheaves, where we promoted the
C∞Set-enrichment of GEmbd to a C∞sSet-enrichment, by taking the constant smooth simplicial set. The model


## Page 43


43
structure exists and is a left proper combinatorial C∞sSet-enriched cartesian model category by Propositions 2.3.10
and 2.3.11, with the model structure on C∞sSet defined in Proposition 2.2.5.
Our definition of a field stack is extremely versatile and captures all significant types of fields we can think of,
including metrics, gauge fields, topological structures, tangential structures, smooth maps to a target manifold,
spinors, superstructures, and Euclidean structures.
In the following examples we work in the setting of Example 4.2.8, taking GCart = C∞Cart, GEmbd = C∞Embd.
Example 5.1.4. Let X be a smooth manifold of any dimension. We can regard X as a field stack via the sheaf
that sends
(M →U) 7→C∞(M, X),
for a submersion M →U. This is clearly a sheaf on C∞Embd since the total space functor C∞Embd →C∞Man,
which maps M →U to M, sends covering families to covering families. This sheaf is not representable.
Example 5.1.5. The previous example can be generalized easily to all simplicial presheaves on the site C∞Man
(or rather, a small subsite of C∞Man that is equivalent to C∞Man). Indeed, the total space functor T: C∞Embd →
C∞Man induces a restriction functor
T ∗: PSh∆(C∞Man)ˇCech →Fieldd,
which manifestly preserves the homotopy descent property.
Example 5.1.6. An example of a field stack that does not come from a simplicial presheaf on smooth manifolds
and smooth maps is given by the presheaf of fiberwise Riemannian metrics. Let
Met: C∞Embop
d →Set
be the presheaf that sends a submersion p: M →U with d-dimensional fibers to the set of metrics on the fiberwise
tangent bundle ker Tp over M. A morphism (a, b): (p: M →U) →(q: N →V ) is sent to the function
(a, b)∗: Met(q) →Met(p)
that sends a metric g on the fiberwise tangent bundle ker Tq to the pullback metric
(a∗g)x(v, w) = ga(x)((Txa)(v), (Txa)(w)),
x ∈M.
This is a well-defined metric on ker Tq since a is a fiberwise open embedding.
We can also consider Riemannian metrics with restrictions on sectional curvature (e.g., positive, negative,
nonpositive, nonnegative), since these properties are preserved by pullbacks along open embeddings. For exam-
ple, we define the subobject of positive sectional curvature metrics Metsc>0 ⊂Met as the functor that sends
p: M →U to the subset of metrics on the fiberwise tangent bundle such that for all u ∈U, the metric g|p−1(u) on
TMu ∼= ker(Tp|Mu) →Mu = p−1(u) is a metric of positive sectional curvature. Such metrics can again be pulled
back by fiberwise open embeddings and the property of having positive sectional curvature is preserved under such
pullbacks.
So far, the examples of field stacks that we have considered have been sheaves of sets (i.e., they do not have
higher morphisms). We now consider the example where the fields are principal bundles with connection, which
incorporates gauge transformations as higher morphisms.
Example 5.1.7. Let G be a Lie group. Consider the field stack GBun∇that sends an object (p: M →U) ∈C∞Embd
to the groupoid GBun∇(p) defined as follows. Objects are pairs (φ: p →q, π: P →N), where (q: N →V ) ∈C∞Embd,
φ = (a: M →N, b: U →V ) is a morphism in C∞Embd, and π: P →N is a principal G-bundle over total space of q
with a fiberwise connection along q. Morphisms
(φ: p →q, π: P →N) →(φ′: p →q′, π: P ′ →N ′)
are given by smooth U-families {τu: πb(u) →πb′(u)}u∈U of connection-preserving morphisms of principal G-bundles
with connection. Composition of morphisms is defined in the obvious way. Given a morphism ψ = (e, f): p′ →p in
C∞Embd, we set
GBun∇(ψ)(φ, π) = (φψ, π),
GBun∇(ψ)({τu}u∈U) = {τf(u′)}u′∈U ′,
which yields a presheaf of groupoids GBun∇on C∞Embd.
Consider the presheaf of groupoids B∇G on C∞Embd that sends an object (p: M →U) ∈C∞Embd to the
groupoid whose objects are trivial principal G-bundles G × M →M with a fiberwise connection (i.e., a vertical 1-
form on the submersion p: M →U with values in g). Morphisms are connection-preserving isomorphisms, specified
as smooth maps M →G. The structure maps for C∞Embd are defined by pulling back vertical 1-forms and smooth

The next example shows that our bordism category is capable of handling the supersymmetric Euclidean field
theories of Stolz–Teichner [2011.a]. Here we will need the full generality of the fibered geometric site ♭: GEmbd →
GCart.
Example 5.1.8. Recall the structured cartesian site C∞SCartC of complex smooth supercartesian spaces (Defini-
tion 4.1.19) and the fibered geometric site C∞SEmb2|1 of 2|1-dimensional complex smooth supermanifolds (Exam-
ple 4.2.11). The reduction functor Re is the usual reduction functor for supermanifolds.
Following Deligne–Freed [1999.f, Section 1.1] and Stolz–Teichner [2011.a, Section 4.2 and (4.10)], we consider the
super Euclidean space E2|1, defined as follows. Let ∆= C be the representation of Spin(2) ∼= U(1) on C given
by θ · z := ¯θz. Let E2|1 be the super Lie group whose underlying cs manifold is R2|1 = R2 × Π∆and whose
multiplication is given in coordinates by
E2|1 × E2|1 →E2|1,
(τ1, ¯τ1, θ1), (τ2, ¯τ2, θ2) 7→(τ1 + τ2, ¯τ1 + ¯τ2 + θ1θ2, θ1 + θ2),
where we are using complex-valued coordinate functions, as in Stolz–Teichner [2011.a, Remark 4.11] (see Defi-
nition 4.1.19 for additional references). The Lie group Spin(2) acts on E2|1 = R2 × Π∆by the twofold cover
Spin(2) →SO(2) and the spinor representation on ∆. We can therefore form the semidirect product E2|1 ⋊Spin(2),
which is the 2|1-super Euclidean group. The semidirect product acts on R2|1 via open embeddings: the first factor
acts via the multiplication in E2|1 and the second factor acts as above.
Now we consider the homotopy quotient
Eucl2|1 := YR2|1→R0//(YE2|1⋊Spin(2) ◦♭op) ∈Field2,
where (R2|1 →R0) ∈C∞SEmb2|1, E2|1 ⋊Spin(2) is the group object in C∞SMan constructed above, promoted
to a presheaf on C∞SCartC via the restricted Yoneda embedding Y and converted to a presheaf on C∞SEmb2|1 by
composing with ♭op.
Given (p: M →U) ∈C∞SEmb2|1, the simplicial set Eucl2|1(p) can be explicitly described as the nerve of the
action groupoid of the action of the group C∞(U, E2|1 ⋊Spin(2)) on the set C∞SEmb2|1(p, R2|1 →R0) that sends
(g: U →E2|1 ⋊Spin(2), φ = (e, b): p →(R2|1 →R0)) 7→((g ◦p) · e, b).
Proceeding similarly to Example 5.1.7, a derived map (p: M →U) →Eucl2|1 can be identified with the following
data.
(1) An open cover {pα: Mα →Uα} of p: M →U.
(2) For each α, a map of supermanifolds eα: Mα →R2|1 such that induced map (e, p): Mα →R2|1 × Uα is an
open embedding.
(3) For each α, β, a map
gαβ: Uαβ = Uα ∩Uβ →E2|1 ⋊Spin(2)
such that gαβ · eα = eβ and such that gαβ satisfies the Čech cocycle condition.
Hence, a derived map (which is an object in the corresponding bordism category) is precisely a U-family of (E2|1 ⋊
Spin(2), R2|1) supermanifolds in the sense of Stolz–Teichner [2011.a, Definition 2.26].

Therefore, it descends to a left Quillen functor between the Čech-local injective model structures.
Example 5.1.11. Applying the isotopification functor of Definition 5.1.9 to the field stack Met ∈PSh∆(C∞Embd)
(Example 5.1.6), we get a field stack with isotopies
Met = IdMet ∈Fieldd = C∞PSh∆(C∞Embd).
By Remark 5.1.10, given p ∈C∞Embd and L ∈C∞Cart, the L-points of the smooth set Met(p: M →U)L are given
by equivalence classes of pairs (g, φ), where g is a fiberwise Riemannian metric on some (q: N →U) ∈C∞Embd,
φ ∈C∞Embd(M →U, N →U)L is a surjective fiberwise open embedding φ: M × L →N covering the projection
map U × L →U, and the equivalence relation is given by (ψ∗g, φ) ∼(g, ψφ), where ψ: (N →U) →(P →U) is
an isomorphism in C∞Embd covering the identity map on U. This smooth set is different from the smooth set of
U ×L-parametrized families of fiberwise Riemannian metrics on M ×L. In particular, the U ×L-families of metrics
in Met are “d-thin” in that the parametrizing map to the moduli stack must factor through a fixed family q of
d-dimensional manifolds.
In the most elementary case, when d = 1 and (p: M →U) = (R×U →U) is the projection map, we can also take
(q: N →U) = (R×U →U) to be the projection map. A fiberwise Riemannian metric on N yields a fiberwise open
embedding σ: N →R induced by the fiberwise distance function. The map σ is unique up to fiberwise translations
by τ: U →R and (in the unoriented case) reflections. By composing φ ∈C∞Emb(p, q)L with σ, we get an L-family
of fiberwise open embeddings of p into R. Furthermore, the smooth set of such open embeddings is a disjoint union
of two smooth sets (corresponding to the two orientations of R), both of which are contractible. The action of
translations and reflections on this smooth set is free. Therefore, the ordinary quotient computes the homotopy
quotient and we have a weak equivalence of smooth simplicial sets
Met(R × U →U) ≃BC∞(U, R),
which is natural in U. Here BC∞(U, R) is the simplicial set given by the delooping of the additive abelian group of
smooth real-valued functions. (With a fiberwise orientation, we get a further factor of BZ/2.) For details on this

in Example 5.1.12. See also Kenig–Pavlov [2026.d] for the case of 1-dimensional Riemannian sigma-models.

5.2. Cuts, cut tuples, and cut grids. In this section, we define the notions of a cut, cut tuple, and cut grid on
an object (p: M →U) ∈C∞Embd, which are responsible for implementing the structure of a symmetric monoidal
d-category for bordisms. The notion of a cut (Definition 5.2.1) is inspired by Stolz–Teichner [2011.a, Definition 2.21].
The notion of a cut grid (Definition 5.2.5) roughly resembles the constructions of Lurie [2009.c, Definition 2.2.9] and
Calaque–Scheimbauer [2015.c, Definition 5.1]. The notion of a globular cut grid is inspired by Henriques [2013.a,
§2.2], who talks about cobordisms with thin parts.
We begin with the notion of a cut on an object p: M →U ∈C∞Embd.
Definition 5.2.1. A cut of an object p: M →U in C∞Embd is a triple (C<, C=, C>) of subsets of M such that there
is a smooth map h: M →R (called the height function) whose fiberwise-regular values form an open neighborhood
of 0 and we have h−1(−∞, 0) = C<, h−1(0) = C=, and h−1(0, ∞) = C>. Here t ∈R is a fiberwise-regular value if
for all m ∈M such that h(m) = t, the map Th|ker T p is surjective. We set
C≤= C< ∪C=,
C≥= C> ∪C=.
We equip the set of cuts with a natural ordering ≤, with C ≤C′ if and only if C< ⊂C′
<. With this ordering, the
set of cuts is a partially ordered set.
We define a functor
(5.2.2)
CutPo: C∞Embop
d →PoSet
as follows.
(1) An object p: M →U is sent to the poset of cuts Cut(p: M →U) on p.
(2) A morphism (f, g): (M →U) →(N →V ) is sent to the order-preserving function
Cut(f, g): Cut(N →V ) →Cut(M →U),
Cut(f, g)(C) = (f, g)∗C = (f −1(C<), f −1(C=), f −1(C>)).
That this is a well defined cut follows from the fact that f is a fiberwise open embedding. In particular, if
h is a height function for the cut C, then h ◦f is a height function for the cut (f, g)∗C.
Composing the functor CutPo with the nerve functor N: PoSet →sSet yields a functor
(5.2.3)
Cut: C∞Embop
d →sSet.
We call an m-simplex in Cut(p: M →U) a cut [m]-tuple C for p: M →U. Thus, a cut [m]-tuple is a collection of
cuts Cj = (C<j, C=j, C>j) of p: M →U indexed by vertices j ∈[m] such that
C0 ≤C1 ≤· · · ≤Cm.
We introduce notation for open and closed regions between cuts, as well as cut tuples obtained by removing some
of the cuts.
Notation 5.2.4. Let p: M →U be an object in C∞Embd. Let C be a cut [m]-tuple. Given j ≤j′, we set
C(j,j′) := C>j ∩C<j′,
C[j,j′] := C≥j ∩C≤j′.
We also denote by C⟨j,j′⟩the cut [j′ −j]-tuple obtained from C by removing the cuts Ck with k < j or k > j′.
Next, we will extend the functor Cut further to a functor
Cutuple
⋔: (∆×d)op × C∞Embop
d →Set,
where the subscript notation will become apparent in a moment.
Morally, Cutuple
⋔
sends a pair consisting of a
multisimplex m and p: M →U in C∞Embd to the set of collections of cut [mi]-tuples, where i ∈{1, . . . , d}.

However, we do not want arbitrary collections of cut tuples. Instead, we only take those cut tuples that intersect
transversally in directions indexed by different elements of {1, . . . , d}. This motivates the following definition.
Definition 5.2.5. Fix d ≥0. We define the cut grid functor
Cutuple
⋔: (∆×d)op × C∞Embop
d →Set
as follows. Let p: M →U be an object in C∞Embd and let m = ([m1], . . . , [md]) ∈∆×d be a multisimplex. The set
Cutuple
⋔(m, p) has elements:
• for each 1 ≤i ≤d, a cut [mi]-tuple Ci on p: M →U,
which satisfy the following transversality property.
⋔. For every subset S ⊂{1, . . . , d} and j: S →Z such that for every i ∈S we have 0 ≤ji ≤mi, there is a
smooth map hj: M →RS such that for every i ∈S, the map
πi ◦hj: M →R,
where πi: RS →R is the ith projection, yields the ji-th cut Ci
ji in the cut tuple Ci, as in Definition 5.2.1.
We require that the fiberwise-regular values of hj form an open neighborhood of 0 in RS.
The structure maps for the ith factor of ∆in ∆×d are given by applying a simplicial map to the ith cut tuple Ci.
We observe that the transversality property is still satisfied after applying a simplicial map, since simplicial maps
simply remove cuts or duplicate cuts, i.e., the function hj in the above definition can be left unchanged. The
structure maps for the factor C∞Embd are given by applying the corresponding structure maps to every cut tuple.
We call the elements of Cutuple
⋔(m, p) cut m-grids.
The condition ⋔in Definition 5.2.5 guarantees that cuts in different simplicial directions intersect transversally.
This is forced by the requirement that the functions hj have the origin as a regular value and by the compatibility
of hj with the cuts. We remind the reader that cuts in the same simplicial direction are allowed to intersect and
even overlap, but are not allowed to intersect transversally (see Figure 2). The next example illustrates this point.

Remark 5.2.8. Recall the following definitions and theorems from the Stacks Project [2025.b].
• (Tag 005O.) A continuous map f: X →Y of topological spaces is proper if it is separated (meaning the
induced map X →X ×Y X is closed) and universally closed (meaning for every continuous Z →Y , the
induced map Z ×Y X →Z is closed).
• (Tag 005R.) A continuous map f is universally closed if and only if f is closed and every fiber of f is a
compact topological space.
• (Tag 0CY3.) A continuous map with a Hausdorff domain is separated.
• (Tag 0CY4.) Separated maps are closed under base change. Therefore, proper maps are closed under base
change.
• If two maps are separated, universally closed, or proper, then their composition has the same property. The
inclusion of a closed subset is a proper map. Restricting a proper map to a closed subset of its domain
again yields a proper map.
Definition 5.2.9. Let C be a cut m-grid for p: M →U. We say that C is compact if for S = {1, 2, . . . , d}, the
restriction of p to C[j,j′] is proper, for all j, j′: S →Z.
The following examples demonstrate that to avoid pathological situations, we must require the restriction of p
to be closed and have compact fibers.
Example 5.2.10. Take (p: M →U) = (R1 →R0). Consider the cut ([1])-grid C, whose only cut tuple is (C1
0, C1
1),
with
C1

Take j = 0 and j′ = 1. The restriction of p to C[j,j′] is the map q: [0, ∞) →R0. The fiber of q at the only point
of R0 is not compact, therefore the map q is not proper. Thus, the cut grid C is not compact.

is empty if x ≤0 and {x} × [1/x, 2/x] if x > 0. However, the map q is not a closed map, since its image is
(0, ∞) ⊂R1 = U, which is not a closed subset of U. Therefore, q is not a proper map and the cut grid C is not
compact.
The notion of a cut m-grid will allow us to define a higher categorical structure on bordisms which is the d-
fold generalization of a double category. A double category is a categorical structure that has objects, horizontal
1-morphisms, vertical 1-morphisms, and 2-cells, whose boundary is a square with two horizontal 1-morphisms and
two vertical 1-morphisms. Having multiple notions of 1-morphisms is an undesirable feature of a bordism category,
since it destroys the properties and constructions pertaining to the notion of dualizability in the setting of the
cobordism hypothesis. To eliminate this issue, we can pass from double categories to bicategories. A bicategory is
a (pseudo) double category in which vertical morphisms are identities, a property also known as globularity. To

Finally, we treat the factor Γ in a manner analogous to ∆×d. Observe that the monoidal structure on bordisms
is given by the disjoint union, and disjoint unions of bordisms can be interpreted as compositions in a direction
where all sources and targets are empty. In our setting, a cut [m]-tuple C = (C0, . . . , Cm) on p: M →U such that
for every i we have C=i = ∅partitions M into disjoint open subsets (Notation 5.2.7):
M = C<0 ⊔C(0,1) ⊔· · · ⊔C(m−1,m) ⊔C>m.
We think of such a cut [m]-tuple as a collection of m bordisms C(0,1), . . . , C(m−1,m), together with two “trash bins”
C<0 and C>m, which are discarded once we pass to a sufficiently small neighborhood of C[0,m]. Such a disjoint
partition can also be encoded by a smooth map M →{−∞, 1, . . . , m, ∞}, which describes almost the same data as
the map C⊗: M →⟨ℓ⟩in Definition 5.2.16 (taking ℓ= m), with two important changes.
• The totally ordered set of intervals
{(−∞, 0), (0, 1), . . . , (m −1, m), (m, ∞)} ∼= {−∞, 1, . . . , m, ∞}
indexing the disjoint bordisms is replaced by the (unordered) pointed finite set
⟨ℓ⟩= {∗, 1, . . . , ℓ}.
• Instead of two trash bins C<0 and C>m indexed by the elements (−∞, 0) and (0, ∞), we have a single trash
bin indexed by the element ∗∈⟨ℓ⟩.
Definition 5.2.16. Fix d ≥0. We define the monoidal cut grid functor
Cutuple
⊗,⋔: Γop × (∆×d)op × C∞Embop
d →Set,

multisimplex. The set Cutuple
⊗,⋔(m, p) has as elements ordered pairs:
• a smooth map C⊗: M →⟨ℓ⟩;
• a cut m-grid (C1, . . . , Cd) ∈Cutuple
⋔(m, p) (Definition 5.2.5).
The structure maps for the factor Γ leave Ci unchanged and compose C⊗: M →⟨ℓ⟩with the given map of pointed
finite sets ⟨ℓ⟩→⟨ℓ′⟩. The structure maps for the factor ∆×d leave C⊗unchanged and for (C1, . . . , Cd) are given in

5.3. Categories of bordisms. In this section, we define two variants of the geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-
category of bordisms: one which includes isotopies for fields (Definition 5.3.16) and one without (Definition 5.3.7).
In both cases, roughly speaking, a composable tuple of k-morphisms with k ≤d is an object p: M →U of GEmbd,
together with a compact globular monoidal cut grid (Definition 5.2.18) that is degenerate in the last (d −k)-
directions, along with a vertex in F(p: M →U), where F ∈Fieldd is a field stack (Definition 5.1.1). In the case
of the isotopy bordism category, we have isotopies of fields (Definition 5.1.3) and compact monoidal cut grids
(Definition 5.2.19) as k-morphisms for k > d. See Remarks 5.3.9 and 5.3.10 for more details.
We begin with a version of the bordism category that allows for noncompact bordisms. This version will be used
as an intermediate step and we will pass to the subobject on compact bordisms.
Definition 5.3.1. Fix d ≥0 and a fibered geometric site GEmbd (Definition 4.2.4), including the site GCart and
the reduction functor Re = Ret: GEmbd →C∞Embd. Recall Fieldd from Definition 5.1.1 and fix F ∈Fieldd. Recall
also the globular monoidal cut grid functor Cut⊗,⋔from Definition 5.2.18. Consider the functor
(5.3.2)
Γop × (∆×d)op →PSh(GEmbd, sSet),
(⟨ℓ⟩, m) 7→((p: M →U) 7→Cut⊗,⋔(⟨ℓ⟩, m, Re p) × F(p)).

Γop × (∆×d)op →PSh(GEmbd, sSet)

Moving the category GCart to the left side using currying yields a functor
(5.3.3)
PreBordF
d : GCartop × Γop × (∆×d)op →Cat∆op,
which we call the geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category of noncompact bordisms with F-structure. The
construction F 7→PreBordF
d is sSet-enriched functorial in F.

d in (5.3.3) has the following explicit description.

d (U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m)k is given by the following.

• Morphisms (p, C, σ) →(q, C′, σ′) are maps φ: p →q ∈GEmbd such that ♭φ = idU, φ∗C′ = C and φ∗σ′ = σ,
where the functor ♭: GEmbd →GCart is given by the data of a fibered geometric site (Definition 4.2.4). We

Proof. This is a direct application of Proposition 4.3.2, taking F to be the functor obtained by evaluating (5.3.2)
at a fixed object (⟨ℓ⟩, m) ∈Γ × ∆×d and [k] ∈∆.
□
Taking GEmbd = C∞Embd and F = 1, for every (U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m) ∈GCart × Γ × ∆×d, the category
C = PreBordF
d (U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m)
has an initial object (p: M →U, C, σ), where M = ∅. Thus, the nerve of C is weakly contractible. The geometric
meaning of this is that morphisms in C need not preserve the core of the monoidal cut grid. This is addressed in the
next proposition, where we construct a subobject CBordd of PreBordd which will be used to construct the bordism
category.
Proposition 5.3.5. Recall Definition 5.3.1 and let PreBordF
d be the functor defined by (5.3.3). Recall the explicit
description of PreBordF

defined by the following conditions.
(c1) On objects (p, C, σ), we require the globular monoidal cut grid C to be compact (Definition 5.2.18).
(c2) On morphisms φ, we require the image of Re φ to contain core(Re p′, C′) (Notation 5.2.17).

Proof. We first prove that CBordF
d (U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m)k is a well defined category.
By Proposition 5.3.4, a morphism
φ: (p, C, σ) →(p′, C′, σ′) is a map φ: p →p′ such that ♭φ = idU, φ∗C′ = C, and φ∗σ′ = σ.
If φ satisfies
Condition (c2), then Im Re φ ⊃core(Re p′, C′). Therefore, the map Re φ restricts to a bijection

core(Re p, C) = core(Re p, φ∗C′)

Clearly identity morphisms satisfy Condition (c2). Suppose we have morphisms
φ: (p, C, σ) →(p′, C′, σ′),
φ′: (p′, C′, σ′) →(p′′, C′′, σ′′)
satisfying Condition (c2). Then the composition φ′φ: p →p′′ satisfies

It remains to show that CBordF
d is a well defined subpresheaf. Hence, we must show that applying a structure
map in GCart × Γ × ∆×d preserves Conditions (c1) and (c2). It suffices to show that Conditions (c1) and (c2) are
preserved by structure maps of the form f × idc, where f is a morphism in one of the factors and c is an object in
the remaining two factors.
Let f be a morphism in Γ. Given an object (p, C, σ), we want to show that that the globular monoidal cut
grid f ∗C is compact.
Denote by M the domain of Re p.
The structure map corresponding to f repartitions
the set of connected components of M and moves some connected components to the trash bin. Thus for each
j, j′, the corresponding subset (f ∗C)⊗,[j,j′] (Notation 5.2.17) is given by removing some connected components of
C⊗,[j,j′]. Hence the restriction of Re p to (f ∗C)⊗,[j,j′] is still proper (Remark 5.2.8), so Condition (c1) is preserved.
Condition (c2) is preserved since core(Re p′, f ∗C′) ⊂core(Re p′, C′) and the embedding φ does not change under
the structure map of f.

Let f be a morphism in ∆×d. We can assume that f is a face or degeneracy map in a single simplicial direction.
Then f either repeats a cut or omits a cut. In both cases, (f ∗C)⊗,[j,j′] ⊂C⊗,[j,j′] is a closed subset for all j, j′. Hence
the restriction of p to (f ∗C)⊗,[j,j′] is proper (Remark 5.2.8) and Condition (c1) is preserved. Again Condition (c2)
is preserved since core(Re p′, f ∗C′) ⊂core(Re p′, C′) and the embedding φ does not change under the structure map
of f.

By assumption, Re p is proper. Since proper maps are closed under base change (Remark 5.2.8), it follows that
Re p′: (f ∗C)⊗,[j,j′] →Re U ′ is also proper.
To verify Condition (c2), suppose a map φ: p →q is a morphism (p, C, σ) →(q, C′, σ′). We have
Im(Re f ∗φ) = (Re ♭∗f)−1(Im(Re φ)) ⊃(Re ♭∗f)−1(core(Re p′, C′)) = core(Re f ∗p′, f ∗C′).
□

d (U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m)k is given by the right image in Figure 7. Changing U, F, ℓ, or m can be visualized by

• Passing from U = R0 to arbitrary U amounts to taking U-parametrized families of bordisms. One can
imagine smoothly varying the right image in Figure 7, along with its cut grid, with respect to the parameter
space U.
• Passing from F = ∗to arbitrary F amounts to adding the data of a field σ ∈F(p)0 on the bordism, such
as a Riemannian metric or a principal G-bundle with connection.
• Passing from ℓ= 1 to arbitrary ℓamounts to labeling the connected components of the bordism with
elements of ⟨ℓ⟩. In Figure 7, one can imagine adding more connected components to the image on the
right in Figure 7 and labeling each connected component with an element of ⟨ℓ⟩. Some components may in
particular be labeled by the trash bin ∗∈⟨ℓ⟩.
• Passing from m = ([2], [4]) to arbitrary multisimplices amounts to changing the number of cuts in the cut
grid. One can imagine adding or removing cuts in the cut grid in Figure 7, according to the multisimplex.

We now provide a conceptual explanation of the k-simplices in Remark 5.3.9. For simplicity, we take F = ∗.
In this case, a k-simplex in Remark 5.3.9 is just a k-simplex in the nerve of the category CBordF
d (U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m) of
Proposition 5.3.5 (hence a composable chain of morphisms). By definition, a morphism must map the cuts in
the cut grid on the domain to corresponding cuts in the cut grid of the codomain. In Figure 7, one can image a
map between two bordisms of the form depicted on the right, where the map must send cuts to corresponding cuts.
Working in a single fiber over the parametrizing space U, such a map is an open embedding on the ambient manifold.
By Condition (c2) in Proposition 5.3.5, the image must contain the core, so on a sufficiently small neighborhood
of the core (in both domain and codomain) this map is a diffeomorphism. Thus the category CBordF
d (U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m)
encodes the groupoid of diffeomorphisms between bordisms.

where [1] is repeated k times.

unoriented bordism category, by taking d = 2, k = 1, U = R0, F = ∗. The ambient manifold is the cylinder
Cz = {(x, y, z) | x2 + y2 = 1}. The cut tuple in the first direction is given by the pair of cuts

The version of the bordism category described in Remark 5.3.9 does not encode the homotopy type of the space
of diffeomorphisms of bordisms like Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss [2006.b] and Lurie [2009.c]. The bordism
category with isotopies, which we now construct, does encode the homotopy type.

In particular, if C is fibrant, then a functorial field theory is simply a morphism BordF

Remark 5.3.19. As explained in Remark 3.1.10, it is neither necessary nor beneficial for symmetric monoidal
(∞, d)-categories to be fibrant in some model structure. A functorial field theory (Definition 1.0.1) is a point in a
derived mapping object R Map(BordF
d , C), which can be presented as a morphism of the form BordF
d →C if BordF
d
is cofibrant and C is fibrant. All objects in the model structure for geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories
(Definition 3.1.8.(1), with or without isotopies) are cofibrant.
Although our bordism categories Bordd and Bordd are not fibrant objects in GCat⊗
∞,d or GCat⊗
∞,d, respectively,
they are local with respect to some of the morphisms in Definition 3.1.8.(1). Since these locality properties are not
useful for the development of functorial field theories, we omit their proofs.
• For a field stack F that satisfies homotopy descent in Fieldd (respectively Fieldd), the category BordF
d (re-
spectively BordF
d ) is local with respect to the Čech morphisms (2.3.18), Segal Γ-maps (2.3.15) and (2.3.16),
Segal ∆-maps (2.3.13).
• If the field stack F does not satisfy homotopy descent, then all three of the above conditions may be
violated. This is the case when F is representable, corresponding to the case of embedded bordism categories
(Section 5.5).
• For any field stack F, the object BordF
d is local with respect to the Segal completion maps (2.3.14). If d ≥5,
and F = ∗, then BordF
d is not local with respect to the completion maps, due to the existence of nontrivial
h-cobordisms.
• Both Bordd and Bordd are local with respect to the globular maps in Definition 3.1.4.
If desired, one could make the following simple adjustments to the definitions of bordism categories, which produce
weakly equivalent bordism categories that satisfy all locality conditions of Definition 3.1.8.(1).
• In the case of Bordd, add h-cobordisms to the smooth simplicial set of objects, as in Lurie [2009.c, Defini-
tion 2.2.10] and Calaque–Scheimbauer [2015.c, Definition 5.24].
• Replace the field stack F by its associated ∞-sheaf RF in all constructions.
We remark that the remaining fibrancy condition, that of injective fibrancy, is typically false unless F = ∅.
Remark 5.3.20. Working with nonfibrant models for the bordism category gives rise to significant simplifications
in the proof of locality of functorial field theories in Grady–Pavlov [2020.e], which uses representable field stacks
(Section 5.5). These stacks do not satisfy descent and give rise to nonfibrant bordism categories. Nonfibrancy
should not be seen as a disadvantage in this case, but rather as an advantage that allows us to work with a much
smaller model for the bordism category in our proofs.

Remark 5.3.22. An objectwise isotopy equivalence of objects in the category
C = PSh(Γ × ∆×d × C∞Embd, C∞Set)/Cutuple
⊗,⋔
of Remark 5.3.21 induces an objectwise weak equivalence of the resulting bordism categories in GCat⊗
∞,d.
Remark 5.3.22 can be used to establish equivalences of various models of bordism categories. For example, it can
be used to define other versions of globular monoidal cut grid functors, e.g., the ones where the strict globularity
condition is relaxed to a cylindrical globularity condition (meaning the condition of being simplicially degenerate
is relaxed to the condition of being isotopic to a simplicially degenerate element), and show them to be weakly
equivalent to the (strictly) globular bordism category with isotopies (Definition 5.3.16). We can also add a specific
choice of height functions to a cut grid, as is commonly done in the literature (Lurie [2009.c, Definition 2.2.9],
Calaque–Scheimbauer [2015.c, Definition 5.1], Schommer-Pries [2017.d, Definition 5.8]).
Conjecture 5.3.23. Taking GCart = 1 and GEmbd = Embd (Example 4.2.20) yields a symmetric monoidal
(∞, d)-category BordF
d that is weakly equivalent to the symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-categories of bordisms of
Calaque–Scheimbauer [2015.c, Definition 9.10] and Schommer-Pries [2017.d, Definition 5.8] with the tangential
structure associated to F ∈Fieldd as constructed in Grady–Pavlov [2026.c, Proposition 7.4].
5.4. Examples of bordism categories. In this section, we provide some examples of bordisms in both Bordd and
Bordd for various types of fields. In each example, we describe an element of the set obtained from Definitions 5.3.7
and 5.3.16 by evaluating at the following parameters:
(1) d ≥0;
(2) ♭: GEmbd →GCart (Definition 4.2.4);
(3) F ∈Fieldd (Definition 5.1.1);
(4) U ∈GCart (Definition 4.1.12);
(5) ⟨ℓ⟩∈Γ;
(6) m ∈∆×d;
(7) [k] ∈∆.
In the case of the isotopy bordism category, we evaluate at
(2) ♭: GEmbd →GCart (Definition 4.2.13);
(3) F ∈Fieldd (Definition 5.1.3);
(8) L ∈C∞Cart.
If we do not set any of the above parameters in an example, we implicitly keep these parameters free. In the case
of the functor ♭, omitting the description means we take the standard smooth geometric structure, as described in
Examples 4.2.8 and 4.2.19.

bordisms.
• We call an element of BordF
d (U, ⟨1⟩, m)1 a U-family of virtual isomorphisms.

The next example shows that in the uple case, manifolds with corners give examples of morphisms in the uple
bordism category (see Remark 6.2.10). This example can be modified to obtain globular bordisms by deforming the
cuts to a globular cut grid, similar to the grid constructed in Example 5.4.3. Such a construction is conceptually

equivalence from the moduli stack A of connected 0-bordisms to the delooping of the sheaf of groups of germs of
L-families of diffeomorphisms Rd →Rd that preserve the basepoint 0, the coordinate hyperplanes xi = 0, and the
coordinate half-spaces xi > 0. The presence of the parameter L allows for a classical argument with a “zoom in
homotopy”, which establishes the contractibility of A.

5.5. Examples of embedded bordisms. In this section, we give examples of bordisms in the case where the field
stack is a representable object. In this case, the field stack does not satisfy homotopy descent. Hence, the bordism
category is not local with respect to the Segal maps. Nevertheless, this example is essential for our formulation
of the axioms (Definitions 1.0.2 and 1.0.5) and our proof of locality and the geometric cobordism hypothesis in
[2020.e, 2021.c].
We begin with the nonisotopy case.

Example 5.5.5. The 1-bordisms η and ϵ described in Example 5.5.2 can almost be identified with the unit and
counit for a duality between the positive and negative point, but not quite. The failure of the duality is a consequence
of the fact that cylinders in the bordism category are not invertible. For example, we can form the composition
that corresponds to one of the triangle identities by composing the 1-bordisms ϵ−1,0 ⊔id+1 and id+−1 ⊔η0,1:

which is not isomorphic to the identity. Moreover, the 0-bordism +−1 is not isomorphic to the 0-bordism +1. They
do become isotopic in the isotopy bordism category via an isotopy that translates from −1 to 1. Likewise, the other
triangle identity also needs such isotopies.
It is precisely this failure that prevents the nonisotopic bordism category Bordd from satisfying the geometric
cobordism hypothesis. However, in the isotopy case, there is an isotopy that contracts the interval to the identity
1-bordism, which implements duality for the 0-bordism +1. We explore the isotopy analog in Example 5.5.7.

degeneration on the cut (x < −1, x = −1, x > 1), implementing the identity 1-bordism on +−1. For the other
triangle identity, replace ϵ−1,0 by the isotopic bordism ϵ1,2 and then form a similar composition.
The next example shows how to compute the trace of a point in the embedded bordism category without explicitly
identifying a fibrant replacement. Although the example shows that it is possible to do this, a more geometrically
appealing calculation of the trace can be obtained by observing that the embedded bordism category is weakly
equivalent to the framed bordism category, as explained in Example 5.5.9.

6. Verification of the axioms
In this section, we will prove that the bordism category without isotopies Bordd (Definition 5.3.7) satisfies Ax-
ioms (A1), (A2), and (A3) (Definition 1.0.2) and that the bordism category with isotopies Bordd (Definition 5.3.16)
satisfies Axioms (A1), (A2), and (A3) (Definition 1.0.5).
Axioms (A1) and (A1) were established in Definitions 5.3.7 and 5.3.16. Section 6.1 proves Axioms (A2) and (A2).
In Section 6.2 we introduce the embedded bordism categories EBordq

A is a fixed set) preserves disjoint unions. Thus, N ∆op ◦CBordd(U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m) preserves objectwise weak equivalences
and tensorings.

and (A2), respectively. The following theorem is a precursor to a much stronger result in Grady–Pavlov [2020.e,
Theorem 6.0.3], which proves a similar statement for sheaves instead of presheaves, meaning Fieldd and Fieldd are
equipped with the Čech-local injective model structure of Definitions 5.1.1 and 5.1.3 instead of the injective model
structure.
Theorem 6.1.3. Fix d ≥0. Recall the categories Fieldd (Definition 5.1.1), Fieldd (Definition 5.1.3), and the model
categories GCat⊗

(Proposition 2.3.1) on PSh∆(GEmbd) and C∞PSh∆(GEmbd), respectively. The functor
Bordd: Fieldd,inj →GCat⊗

Proof. Recall (Proposition 2.3.1) that in an injective model structure the class of cofibrations coincides with the
class of monomorphisms. In particular, all objects are cofibrant.
By Proposition 6.1.1, the functor Bordd is an sSet-enriched left adjoint functor that preserves monomorphisms
and objectwise weak equivalences. Since all objects in Fieldd,inj are cofibrant and weak equivalences are objectwise
weak equivalences, the functor Bordd is an sSet-enriched left Quillen functor.
By Proposition 6.1.2, the functor Bordd is an C∞sSet-enriched left adjoint functor that preserves monomorphisms
and objectwise weak equivalences. Since all objects in Fieldd,inj are cofibrant and weak equivalences are objectwise
weak equivalences, the functor Bordd is an C∞sSet-enriched left Quillen functor.
□

6.2. Proof of the third axiom. Theorem 6.1.3 reduces the study of bordism categories BordF
d and BordF
d with
arbitrary field stacks F to the study of bordism categories BordYp:M→U
d
and BordYp:M→U
d
with representable field
stacks Yp:M→U, meaning bordisms are embedded into a fixed d-dimensional manifold M, or a family thereof. In this
section, we simplify the description of the resulting bordism category further and refer to it as the embedded bordism
category. Variants of nonextended embedded bordism categories have appeared in the literature previously, see, for
example, Galatius–Madsen–Tillmann–Weiss [2006.b, Section 2], Galatius–Randal-Williams [2009.c, Definition 3.8].
The main advantage of this category is that it has no nontrivial virtual isomorphisms. That is, the embedded
bordism category is just a presheaf of sets (or smooth sets in the isotopy case), as opposed to a simplicial presheaf
(or a smooth simplicial presheaf in the isotopy case). Another advantage is that the ambient manifolds of bordisms
are open subsets of a d-dimensional manifold, which can eventually be taken to be Rd. This simplifies the cutting
and gluing constructions in the sequel papers [2020.e, 2021.c].
A key observation (see the proof of Proposition 6.2.4) enabling the simplification of the description of BordYp:M→U
d
and BordYp:M→U
d
is that the nerve of the category CBordYp:M→U
d
(U, ⟨ℓ⟩, m) is homotopy 0-truncated, hence weakly
equivalent to its set of connected components. These connected components can be explicitly described as germs of
the image of the core in M, which motivates the definition of an embedded bordism (Definition 6.2.1). Thus, the
comparison map from BordYp:M→U
d
to the embedded bordism category is conceptually simple: it sends a bordism
equipped with a fiberwise embedding into p to the germ of the image of its core under the embedding into p.
We begin by defining the embedded bordism category functors. The following definition has the advantage of
being concise and having all the details by virtue of building upon the existing Definitions 5.3.7 and 5.3.16. However,
it is not as simple as the isomorphic construction in Proposition 6.2.5, which does not make use of any constructions
in Section 5.3 and instead defines the embedded bordism category directly in terms of compact globular monoidal
cut grids.
Fix d ≥0 and a fibered geometric site GEmbd (Definition 4.2.4) or a fibered geometric site with isotopies GEmbd
(Definition 4.2.13). Recall also the functors Cut⊗,⋔(Definition 5.2.18) and Cut⊗,⋔(Definition 5.2.19), as well as the
notation core(p, C) (Notation 5.2.17).
Definition 6.2.1. Fix d ≥0. Recall the functors Bordd: Fieldd →GCat⊗
∞,d (Definition 5.3.7) and Bordd: Fieldd →

We now prove that the comparison maps eq and eq in Definition 6.2.1 are weak equivalences.
Definition 6.2.2. A category C is weakly cofiltered if every finite connected diagram G: I →C admits a cone. A
presheaf D of categories on C∞Cart (Definition 2.1.8.(2)) is locally weakly cofiltered if for every L ∈C∞Cart and
finite connected diagram G: I →D(L) there is a covering family {fi: Ui →L}i∈I such that for every i ∈I the
diagram D(fi) ◦G: I →D(Ui) admits a cone.
Proposition 6.2.3. If C ∈Cat is weakly cofiltered (Definition 6.2.2), then the canonical map NC →π0NC is a
simplicial weak equivalence. If D ∈CatC∞Cartop is locally weakly cofiltered, then the canonical map ND →˜π0ND is
a weak equivalence in C∞sSet. Here (˜π0F)(L) = π0(F(L)) is the presheaf of connected components of F.

is weakly contractible, the first claim follows. For the second claim, observe that the map
ND →˜π0(ND)
is a stalkwise weak equivalence, equivalently, a Čech-local weak equivalence (Jardine [2015.a, Section 4.1], see also
Pavlov [2022.b, Proposition 12.5]), and hence a weak equivalence in C∞sSet.
□

To prove the final claim, suppose b, b′ ∈C are identified by the quotient map C →π0C. This means that b and
b′ are connected by a chain of morphisms (in either direction) in the preorder C. Inserting identities if necessary,
we get a chain of zigzags b = b0 ←b1 →b2 ←b3 →b4 ←· · · ←b2k = b′. Since C has pullbacks, we can compose
the chain of zigzags to a single zigzag, i.e., a span b ←b1 →b′.
Likewise, if b, b′ ∈D(L) are identified by the quotient map D(L) →π0D(L), then they are connected by a chain
of zigzags b = b0 ←b1 →b2 ←b3 →b4 ←· · · ←b2k = b′. As shown above, for every index i ∈(0, k) there is an
open cover {Uj}j∈J of L such that for every j ∈J the restriction of the cospan b2i−1 →b2i ←b2i+1 to Uj admits
a cone. By passing to the common refinement of these open covers, we get a single open cover {Vk}k∈K such that
for every k ∈K we have a single zigzag b ←b1 →b′ connecting the restrictions of b and b′ to Vk.
□

Indeed, by assumptions on τ1 and τ2 we have ♭τ1 = ♭τ2 = idU, C = (Re τ1)∗C1 = (Re τ2)∗C2, σ1 ◦τ1 = σ = σ2 ◦τ2,

This completes the verification of Axioms (A3) and (A3). We are now ready to complete the proof of Theo-
rems 1.0.4 and 1.0.6.
Theorem 6.2.8. There exists a geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category of bordisms (Definition 1.0.2),
unique up to a weakly contractible choice. Furthermore, the bordism category Bordd (Definition 5.3.7) satisfies
Definition 1.0.2.
Proof. Axiom (A1) was established in Definition 5.3.7. By Theorem 6.1.3, Axiom (A2) is satisfied. By Proposi-
tion 6.2.4, the map e in Definition 6.2.1 is a weak equivalence. This proves Axiom (A3).
For the uniqueness part, observe that Fieldd is a homotopy cocompletion of GEmbd. Therefore, the relative
category of homotopy cocontinuous functors Fieldd →GCat⊗
∞,d equipped with a weak equivalence F ◦Y →EBordd
is weakly contractible.
□
Theorem 6.2.9. There exists a geometric symmetric monoidal (∞, d)-category with isotopies of bordisms (Defini-
tion 1.0.5), unique up to a weakly contractible choice. Furthermore, the bordism category Bordd (Definition 5.3.16)
satisfies Definition 1.0.5.


## Page 77


77
Proof. Axiom (A1) was established in Definition 5.3.16.
By Theorem 6.1.3, Axiom (A2) is also satisfied.
By
Proposition 6.2.4, the map e in Definition 6.2.1 is a weak equivalence. This proves Axiom (A3).
For the uniqueness part, observe that Fieldd is a homotopy cocompletion of GEmbd. Therefore, the relative
category of homotopy cocontinuous functors F: Fieldd →GCat⊗
∞,d equipped with a weak equivalence F ◦Y →EBordd
is weakly contractible.
□
Remark 6.2.10. The multiple versions of the bordism category are defined by using the uple cut grid functors
of Definitions 5.2.16 and 5.2.19, as explained in Remark 5.3.21. We remark that the proof of the main theorem
also works in the uple case. In more detail, there is an obvious axiomatization of the uple category of bordisms
with the same Axioms (A1) and (A2) and with Axiom (A3) obtained by removing the word globular. The proof of
Axiom (A3) in this section carries through verbatim in the uple case.
